00c5c0ae-e125-4d6d-8fbb-a4d24e1ebcfb	Checker Motors Corporation	Every classic yellow-and-black checkered New York City taxi ever built came out of a factory in Kalamazoo, Michigan — Checker Motors moved there from Chicago in 1923 and built the cars until production ended in 1982.
00e17488-7bc5-42a9-befb-a81f35e29cae	Nintendo	Nintendo is far older than its games suggest — founded in Kyoto in 1889 by Fusajiro Yamauchi to make hand-painted 'hanafuda' playing cards. It sold cards (and at various points ran love hotels, a taxi company and instant rice) for decades before its first video game, and it still sells hanafuda today.
01c4ab61-f0a8-4f36-9e81-adb2af47cbda	BERSERK	From Old Norse *berserkr* — "bear-shirt" (*ber-* + *serkr*), referring to elite Viking warriors who wore bear pelts into battle in a trance-like frenzy.
034329dc-2a5d-4a7f-be72-a8924f661cf9	Woody Guthrie	The lyrics of Dropkick Murphys' "I'm Shipping Up to Boston" — the platinum single that opens The Departed — are a five-line unpublished Woody Guthrie fragment about a sailor hunting for his lost wooden leg. Guthrie's daughter Nora invited singer Ken Casey into the Guthrie archives in New York (white gloves required), where he pulled the scrap from thousands of never-set-to-music pages; the band already had the riff and no words.
03bd79c5-b7dc-4233-abe2-f9bc2c51ed06	ASSASSIN'S CREED	"Assassin" comes from the Nizari Ismaili sect founded by Hasan-i Sabbah in 1090, running political killers from the mountain fortress of Alamut in Persia during the Crusades. The popular "hashish-eater" origin story was invented in 1809 by a French orientalist, unsupported by contemporary Muslim sources.
0426cfc3-776e-4952-86a4-ed95e4a9edef	Matt Damon	For Saving Private Ryan, Spielberg put the whole squad — Hanks, Sizemore, Vin Diesel, Barry Pepper and co. — through Capt. Dale Dye's brutal six-day boot camp (5-mile runs, three hours' sleep, cold rain), and deliberately exempted ONE actor: Damon stayed home so the cast's resentment of him — and of Private Ryan — would be real on camera.
0432f539-74af-49df-b6b0-7014772b2df3	Harry Chiti	On April 25, 1962, the Cleveland Indians traded catcher Harry Chiti to the fledgling New York Mets for a player to be named later — then on June 15 the Mets sent that 'player to be named later' back to Cleveland: Harry Chiti himself. He remains the first of only four players in MLB history ever traded for himself.
0472d669-cb15-4cdc-893a-a44342e2d776	Soo Locks	Sault Ste. Marie's Soo Locks — the passage that lets Great Lakes freighters move between Lake Superior and the lower lakes — carried so much of the country's iron ore during WWII that the Army made it the most heavily defended inland site in the U.S.: roughly 7,300 troops, 48 anti-aircraft guns, 51 barrage balloons on steel cables to snag dive bombers, and underwater steel netting to stop torpedoes, all guarding a stretch of the St. Marys River most Americans have never heard of.
048a7282-8f28-47f6-882a-216526623361	The Snurfer (Sherman Poppen, Muskegon)	The modern snowboard traces to Christmas Day 1965 in Muskegon, Michigan, when Sherman Poppen bolted two children's skis together so his daughters could 'surf' the backyard snow dunes. His wife Nancy named it the 'Snurfer' (snow + surfer), and Brunswick Corporation — the Muskegon bowling-equipment company — mass-produced it using leftover bowling-lane wood laminate.
04e92cb6-2135-48e7-9847-648a5e0ba2de	Heineken	After seeing bottles littering Curaçao's beaches in 1960, Alfred Heineken commissioned architect John Habraken to design "a brick that holds beer" — the WOBO (World Bottle), a square-sided interlocking beer bottle meant to double as a building brick for cheap housing. About 100,000 were made at Leerdam in 1963–64 before the company killed it (liability worries, and fear of associating the brand with poverty). Only two WOBO structures were ever built, both on/at Heineken's own property, and surviving bottles now sit in the Victoria and Albert Museum.
05cd465d-e2d9-4e27-ae93-589f416cb55c	quiz	The famous origin story of the word "quiz" — that Dublin theater manager Richard Daly won a 1791 bet that he could inject a nonsense word into the language within ~48 hours by hiring urchins to chalk QUIZ on every wall in Dublin — is itself a fabrication. The word is attested by 1780 (meaning "an odd person"), a decade before the supposed bet, and the earliest telling of the Daly story only appears in 1835. The origin story of "quiz" is a hoax; the word's real origin is unknown.
066f9d7a-46f6-4ea0-9665-4d8c6918f1a3	Phil LaMarr	The guy who gets accidentally shot in the face by John Travolta in Pulp Fiction ("Marvin") is also the voice of Samurai Jack and Green Lantern/John Stewart in Justice League.
06965b5b-53c1-49c1-ba7f-41dcc3c6e790	SWEETHEARTS (conversation hearts)	There were no Sweethearts for Valentine's Day 2019 — NECCO went bankrupt in 2018 after an FDA citation for rodent infestation, and the brand sat dark for a year until Spangler Candy bought it.
06dafbc8-0145-40fb-86a8-9c40e4c19db8	Goodyear	Charles Goodyear discovered vulcanized rubber by accident in 1839, dropping a rubber-and-sulfur mixture on a hot stove — he died broke in 1860. The Goodyear Tire & Rubber Company wasn't founded by him: Frank Seiberling started it in 1898, 38 years after Goodyear's death, naming it in his honor.
07021a4e-bc03-44a6-b90e-e77570fb67fb	DEL SHANNON	Born in Coopersville, Michigan, hit #1 with "Runaway" (1961) — its warbling solo isn't a guitar or organ, it's the "Musitron," a homemade electronic keyboard built by bandmate Max Crook, modeled on the Clavioline, predating most commercial synthesizers.
08fe09da-75b5-4bec-8043-3bb02a376e69	Boxing ring	A boxing "ring" has been square since 1838, when the London Prize Ring Rules first codified a roped-off square fighting area — but the name never changed, because it descended from the literal circle of ground (and circle of spectators) that bare-knuckle fights used before ropes and posts existed.
0903e302-d6e6-4ea3-81de-139cd1e88841	bra	In 2009 Dr. Elena Bodnar won a real Ig Nobel Prize in Public Health for inventing a bra that converts, in under five seconds, into two protective gas masks. She developed it after treating Chernobyl fallout victims and finding inhaled particles caused as much harm as the radiation itself.
0904b370-8865-4998-b470-6a9927dfd410	The Lone Ranger	He was born in Michigan — created by George Trendle and Fran Striker at Detroit radio station WXYZ, debuting January 30, 1933. The same station and creators launched the Green Hornet in 1936, and canonically the Green Hornet (Britt Reid) is the Lone Ranger's great-nephew — established on-air in a 1947 episode.
090b820d-883f-43b8-a890-6c2a54ee20a1	Grape-Nuts	Grape-Nuts — invented 1897 in Battle Creek, Michigan by C.W. Post, a former patient of his rival Dr. Kellogg's sanitarium — became the unofficial cereal of expeditions: it sponsored Admiral Byrd's 1933 Antarctic expedition (route maps printed on the boxes), went into the pre-1944 US jungle ration in WW2, and was packed along on Hillary and Norgay's 1953 first ascent of Everest, which Post advertised for decades.
096e243d-fea4-4e94-a4dd-5aa1f8dd5fbd	FIGHT CLUB	Before Tyler Durden is formally introduced, single subliminal frames of him flash almost imperceptibly in earlier scenes — a self-referential joke, since Tyler's own job in the film is a projectionist who splices porn frames into family movies.
09e6f387-2a1c-40cb-a3a0-6712772fb4d5	Detroit Tigers	On May 18, 1912, the Tigers staged baseball's first player strike — protesting Ty Cobb's suspension for climbing into the stands to beat a disabled heckler. Facing a $5,000 forfeit fine, Detroit fielded a team of Philadelphia amateurs recruited on the spot. Pitcher Allan Travers — a St. Joseph's College student who had never played organized ball — threw a complete game, surrendering 24 runs on 26 hits, both still AL records, in a 24–2 loss to the A's. Travers was ordained in 1926: the only Catholic priest ever to play Major League Baseball.
0a08e0c5-aee9-4da8-ae85-770b0fee4e06	Stone Temple Pilots	Before landing on "Stone Temple Pilots," the band was called "Mighty Joe Young" until they learned a real blues musician already had that name. Scrambling for a replacement, they built the band name backward from childhood nostalgia for STP Motor Oil stickers.
0a377064-b57b-4c5c-82e5-0dffc13d5f83	JEEP	The Jeep's name has two competing origin stories still argued today: military slang for "GP" (General Purpose), or "Eugene the Jeep" — a magical do-anything Popeye comic-strip creature debuting 1936.
0b593917-6f36-4370-aefd-354c7c7645bd	John Adams (and Thomas Jefferson)	John Adams and Thomas Jefferson — the Declaration's principal author and its fiercest floor advocate, later bitter rivals turned lifelong pen pals — died within about five hours of each other on July 4, 1826, the document's 50th anniversary. Adams' last words were 'Thomas Jefferson survives,' not knowing Jefferson had already died at Monticello that afternoon. The 200th anniversary of that coincidence fell on July 4, 2026.
0cc4eef1-5153-4aa1-9e0a-d3ae93806ef6	THE KILLERS	Brandon Flowers took the name off a bass drum in New Order's 2001 "Crystal" music video — a fictional mimed band's kit reads "The Killers."
0cf51fc5-ec5c-4e6e-82db-040b10807084	EMU	November 1932, the Australian military deployed WWI veterans with two Lewis machine guns against ~20,000 emus overrunning Western Australian wheat farms. After 45 days, soldiers had killed only ~2,500 birds and the government called off the "Emu War" — the emus effectively won.
101c8c12-5f53-4877-8b3e-02ec25b01590	Mulholland Drive	David Lynch's film — the one the BBC's critics poll named the best film of the 21st century — started life as a Twin Peaks spin-off about Audrey Horne moving to Hollywood (confirmed by co-creator Mark Frost and Sherilyn Fenn), then became a 90-minute ABC TV pilot that the network killed; French money from StudioCanal let Lynch reshoot an ending, and the salvaged pilot won him Best Director at Cannes 2001.
10b83bac-5fd4-42db-a167-af3a9d658453	Milton Bradley	Milton Bradley was a Springfield lithographer whose best-selling print was a portrait of clean-shaven Abraham Lincoln — until Lincoln grew his beard after the 1860 election run-up, customers demanded refunds, and Bradley burned his worthless inventory. Nearly ruined, he pivoted that same winter to printing a board game, The Checkered Game of Life (1860) — complete with squares for Prison, Ruin, and Suicide — and sold 45,000+ copies by 1861, founding the game empire that later gave us Twister and Operation.
115ff54c-1ed9-4bbf-9f93-272643f61c71	MONGOOSE	Carries a genetic mutation in its acetylcholine receptors that blocks snake neurotoxins — one of only a handful of mammal lineages naturally immune to cobra venom.
11db0356-457f-4132-b145-8d2b3902e5f6	TETRIS	Invented 1984 by Soviet programmer Alexey Pajitnov — but since he worked for the state, rights belonged to Soviet agency Elorg. A 1989 Moscow bidding war ended with Nintendo winning handheld rights and bundling it with every Game Boy sold in North America. Pajitnov saw no royalties until rights reverted to him in 1996.
12e98f17-6a61-4d29-bc8a-1901a78493f1	NAKED MOLE-RAT	Almost entirely immune to cancer, lives up to 37 years — longest lifespan of any rodent, no rise in mortality with age.
1329517b-e2e3-4948-9802-073ac40efe5e	Woody Strode	Toy Story's Sheriff Woody was named as a tribute to this man, who helped integrate the NFL in 1946, a year before Jackie Robinson broke baseball's color line, and later starred in Westerns like The Man Who Shot Liberty Valance.
1357c83d-85d0-4d27-a623-46af3a3eb4e8	Al Pacino	Al Pacino won a Razzie for playing a fictionalized version of himself in Adam Sandler's Jack and Jill, rapping about a fake Dunkin' Donuts drink called "Dunkaccino." He revealed in his 2024 memoir he took the role because he'd just lost his life savings to a Ponzi scheme.
136b0cfd-f243-42ee-aa09-050f12eceda9	FENWAY PARK	The lone red seat in Fenway's green bleachers (Sec. 42, Row 37, Seat 21) marks where Ted Williams' 502-foot homer landed June 9, 1946 — still the park's longest — striking napping fan Joseph Boucher, punching a hole through his straw hat.
13a4ad73-e879-4823-8129-849007dd9921	Jim Abbott	On September 4, 1993, Yankees lefty Jim Abbott — born without a right hand — threw a no-hitter against a loaded Cleveland lineup featuring Albert Belle, Manny Ramirez, Jim Thome and Kenny Lofton. He fielded by switching his glove onto his right arm after each pitch. It remains the only no-hitter thrown by a pitcher born with one hand.
141eaf45-c8e0-4505-a90e-e3efea44a28c	Cleveland	In 1986, Cleveland's United Way tried to set a Guinness world record by releasing 1.5 million balloons over downtown ("Balloonfest '86") — it worked (still the largest mass balloon release ever recorded), but the balloons collided with a rainstorm, dropped while still inflated, shut down a runway at Burke Lakefront Airport, and blanketed Lake Erie during a Coast Guard search for two missing fishermen who were later found drowned. Widely reported at the time as connected; a Coast Guard search-and-rescue program manager stated in a 2024 interview the balloons "did not have anything to do with" the deaths — treat the causation as disputed, not settled.
1422ddf3-16c4-480d-adbd-03a987946dff	Disco Demolition Night	The Detroit Tigers own the last forfeit win in American League history — awarded 9–0 on July 12, 1979, when Chicago DJ Steve Dahl's "Disco Demolition Night" (98-cent admission if you brought a disco record to be blown up, a nod to WLUP 97.9) drew ~50,000 to Comiskey Park; the between-games explosion and ~7,000 field-storming rioters wrecked the field, and game two of the doubleheader was never played.
147141e5-d87b-4680-a00c-09b099a4da97	TWENTY ONE PILOTS	Tyler Joseph named the band after Arthur Miller's 1947 play *All My Sons*, where a WWII defense manufacturer ships cracked engine parts, getting 21 pilots killed.
1480e595-1da6-4724-98b6-55bb38e3a88c	Linus Pauling	Chemist Linus Pauling is the only person ever to win two Nobel Prizes on his own, unshared — Chemistry in 1954, for work on the chemical bond, then Peace in 1962, for anti-nuclear-testing activism.
14c299c2-dc8a-4f03-a43b-48a597258ac1	Winnie the Pooh	When Jeremy Irons' voice gave out recording Scar's "Be Prepared" for The Lion King (1994), Disney had the voice of Winnie the Pooh finish the villain song — Jim Cummings (Pooh AND Tigger since the late '80s, credited in the film as Ed the hyena) sang the final third of the track doing a seamless Irons impression; he calls the gig "stunt singing." The switch lands around "the sensational news."
150e7888-1321-4221-9180-765e0b913b50	AXOLOTL	Fully regrows limbs, jaw, heart tissue, spinal cord, even brain tissue — repeatedly, no scarring. Never metamorphoses; survives naturally only in Xochimilco canals under Mexico City, critically endangered.
15263c67-4526-4ae8-b674-205fe6d8f5ea	MICHIGAN STATE SPARTANS	Before every home game, players/coaches walk from Kellogg Center to Spartan Stadium and toss pennies at the Spartan statue's base for luck. The 1945 original was cast in terra-cotta (WWII bronze rationing); a 2005 bronze replica stands outside now.
152fc406-b6d9-4b59-8076-1ad258ff996b	Slogan	"Slogan" comes from Scottish Gaelic "sluagh-ghairm" — literally "army cry," a Highland clan's rallying shout — entering English by the 1670s, not taking its modern advertising sense until the 1700s.
15798b3c-f7e2-40d8-97ce-1342a3ddf293	Tug of War	Tug of war was an official medal event at the Summer Olympics from 1900 through 1920, then dropped when the IOC trimmed the program. Its biggest scandal came in 1908 in London: the Liverpool police team competed in their standard-issue service boots, the U.S. team protested the footwear as illegal, the protest was denied, and the Americans withdrew in disgust rather than face a rematch.
15b3fcde-7b7a-4688-811a-a9b17a2b6296	Rodriguez (Sixto Rodriguez)	Detroit folk-rock musician Rodriguez released two albums in the early 1970s that flopped completely in the US — he spent decades doing manual labor and construction in Detroit, unaware that, without his knowledge, his records had become massively popular in apartheid-era South Africa, where he's said to have outsold Elvis Presley and was rumored (falsely) to have died by suicide onstage.
1644cf15-c906-4d77-aaac-9d82a317f484	Fanta	Fanta was invented in Nazi Germany in 1940 as a Coca-Cola workaround. When a U.S. trade embargo cut off Coca-Cola syrup to its German subsidiary, plant head Max Keith told his team to use their 'Fantasie' (imagination) to make something from whatever scraps were on hand — apple pomace, whey, beet sugar — and a salesman shouted back 'Fanta!' It sold three million cases in Germany in 1943 alone.
17494f2c-828a-41b3-b185-60bd81dab053	Africa	Africa is slowly splitting in two along the East African Rift — the Somali and Nubian tectonic plates are pulling apart at about 8-9mm/year — and geologists expect the rift to eventually flood and become Earth's sixth ocean, tens of millions of years from now.
18146c4e-8509-4fe5-81ff-01b6ff023b63	NIAGARA FALLS	In 1896, hosted the world's first large-scale long-distance AC transmission — a Tesla/Westinghouse system sending power 20 miles to Buffalo, kickstarting the modern power grid. Its "Honeymoon Capital of the World" title dates to 1803, when Napoleon's brother Jerome Bonaparte honeymooned there.
18427f0c-86b6-4208-92b7-5722e8fbf0a6	DETROIT RED WINGS	April 15, 1952, brothers Pete and Jerry Cusimano (Detroit fish market owners) hurled an octopus onto the Olympia Stadium ice — 8 tentacles symbolizing the 8 playoff wins then needed for the Cup. The Wings won that night, and the tradition stuck.
184db164-bc8d-4344-956b-27127081d8f2	Woodrow Wilson	Woodrow Wilson is the only U.S. president to hold an earned doctorate — a Ph.D. in history and political science from Johns Hopkins, 1885. He was president of Princeton University before he was President of the United States.
188ca300-a0c8-4ffc-b682-067f1ef1024b	Super Bowl I	It's the only Super Bowl broadcast on two networks at once (CBS and NBC) — yet no complete recording survives, because both networks wiped their tapes to reuse them, reportedly recording soap operas over the game. A partial CBS tape surfaced in a Wilkes-Barre, PA attic in 2011; the NFL finally re-aired the game in 2016 by stitching together all 145 plays from NFL Films footage over the radio call.
1a652cfe-a6df-4f87-94ae-5e7d0096dc7b	Toronto Raptors	Toronto's 1995 NBA expansion team is named for the velociraptors of Jurassic Park (1993). The original uniform was a busy purple design with a red dinosaur — fans immediately nicknamed it the "Barney jersey" after the friendly purple TV dinosaur, even though the Raptors' dino was red, not purple.
1aadc426-4399-4fcb-8761-209e2c386c45	Snow White and the Seven Dwarfs	Walt and Roy Disney mortgaged their houses and took out multiple Bank of America loans to finish the $1.49M film, which Hollywood insiders derisively nicknamed "Disney's Folly" during production, certain a 90-minute cartoon would flop.
1b5b32dd-0cec-4b94-9404-5efa65148c5d	Pisa	The Leaning Tower of Pisa's tilt comes from soft, unstable subsoil under one side of its foundation; a 1990s stabilization project corrected the lean from over 5.5 degrees to about 4 degrees, rather than fully straightening it.
1bffb3aa-ee0d-4c93-ae03-d339da8c5f2c	CEREAL	"Cereal" comes from Ceres, the Roman goddess of agriculture and harvest — entered English in 1832 for "grain-yielding grass." Not applied to breakfast food until 1899, nearly 70 years later.
1c7b51d1-4029-4d72-b3b0-7c4d09b8ec26	BOMBARDIER BEETLE	When threatened, mixes stored chemicals in an internal chamber, triggering a near-100°C, ~70-pulse-per-second chemical explosion sprayed with pinpoint aim at attackers.
1c876d4c-6203-48d3-bf35-9629a0e96825	Fernando Tatis	On April 23, 1999, Fernando Tatis hit two grand slams in the SAME inning — both off the same pitcher, the Dodgers' Chan Ho Park — driving in a record eight runs in one inning. Nobody had ever done it, and Park set his own record for allowing both.
1c8a5dae-cbe4-4f22-b84c-afe828493488	Alan Shepard	When Apollo 14's Alan Shepard hit two golf balls on the Moon in 1971, he claimed on live TV his second shot went "miles and miles and miles." In 2021, imaging specialist Andy Saunders matched original photos against modern satellite imagery to find the shots actually traveled just 24 and 40 yards.
1cb4e489-106a-4478-94f7-1345ab73dfdc	Michael Moore	In 1998 Columbia Pictures sued Miramax because the poster and trailer for Moore's documentary The Big One dressed him as a Man in Black — black suit, sunglasses, NYC skyline, oversized microphone in place of the oversized gun — under the tagline "Protecting the Earth from the scum of corporate America." A federal judge granted Columbia a preliminary injunction: parodying corporate America with the MIB campaign wasn't fair use, and the ads were pulled.
1cbc7833-08f1-43de-a08f-bc7cefa50c7e	Quark	Physicist Murray Gell-Mann named the subatomic particle "quark" in the 1960s after "Three quarks for Muster Mark" in James Joyce's Finnegans Wake — he'd already settled on the sound ("kwork") and found Joyce's spelling by chance.
1cec8bbb-d836-485f-9d27-8c5b60be5971	D.B. Cooper	On November 24, 1971, a man calling himself "Dan Cooper" hijacked a Boeing 727, extorted $200,000, and parachuted out over the Pacific Northwest — never identified. The FBI formally closed the case in July 2016; it remains the only unsolved act of air piracy in American aviation history.
1d545f21-c231-4648-b30b-e73336be40bc	WILT CHAMBERLAIN	His 100-point game (March 2, 1962) happened at Hershey Sports Arena in Hershey, PA, not Philadelphia — the Warriors played some "home" games there to build fan base. No video survives; only a partial 4th-quarter radio broadcast.

1f950e78-5783-4573-b2ea-4f0de8a0f4cd	Etch A Sketch	The Etch A Sketch began as a shop-floor accident in 1950s France. Electrical technician Andre Cassagnes was installing a plastic light-switch decal at a wallpaper factory when he noticed it transferred an image to the surface behind it. He built a prototype he called 'L'Ecran Magique' ('the magic screen'), unveiled it at a 1959 toy fair, then licensed it to the Ohio Art Company, which launched it in the U.S. for Christmas 1960.

21cad863-8936-4fa1-be1e-4a8f01d4f0c9	Nine Inch Nails	"Old Town Road" — 19 weeks at No. 1, the longest run in Billboard Hot 100 history — is built on a banjo loop Lil Nas X bought in a $30 beat that samples NIN's ambient track "34 Ghosts IV" (2008). Trent Reznor and Atticus Ross got songwriting credits and ended up CMA Award nominees; Reznor: "I didn't see that one coming."
22496619-2418-42a3-86ad-bfd3b1e49c28	A&W Root Beer	The name is the founders' last-name initials — Roy Allen and Frank Wright started as a Lodi, California root beer stand in 1919. 2249cd5a-0ce2-44a4-b569-41d61e1f4952	SIDEBURNS	"Sideburns" is a flipped corruption of "burnsides," named for Union Civil War general Ambrose Burnside, whose facial hair connected cheek whiskers to a mustache with a clean-shaven chin. Newspapers called it "Burnside whiskers" as early as 1866.
229c2370-786d-4728-bf4c-fe22a85d70f5	TEDDY BEAR	Nov. 1902, a Clifford Berryman cartoon showed Teddy Roosevelt refusing to shoot a tethered bear. Brooklyn candy shop owner Morris Michtom sewed a stuffed bear and put it in his window as "Teddy's Bear."
22cba681-f3e1-4c3f-8954-da7ccb6538dd	KOMODO DRAGON	Largest living lizard species (males up to 10 ft, 330 lbs). Long thought to kill via infectious saliva bacteria; 2009 MRI/venom-gland research proved a genuine venomous bite disrupting blood clotting.
23780c19-a027-4949-84d1-4e10ce0b14f9	Listerine	Listerine is named after Dr. Joseph Lister, pioneer of antiseptic surgery — it launched in the 1880s as a surgical disinfectant, not mouthwash. Decades later, ad man Gerald Lambert coined the word "halitosis" (Latin "halitus" = breath) to market it as bad-breath medicine, juicing sales from $100,000 to $4 million (1921–1927).
24e6e401-f881-4d2c-b23f-2450201f5979	Play-Doh	Play-Doh spent its first life as a wallpaper cleaner. Cincinnati's Kutol made a soft putty for rubbing coal soot off wallpaper; when cleaner home heating killed that market in the 1950s, a nursery-teacher relative realized children could mold it. Rebranded Play-Doh in 1956, it has since sold over three billion cans.
253c0fe8-6451-498f-bafd-de4c009e6d2c	DOUGHNUT	WWI Salvation Army volunteers Ensign Margaret Sheldon and Adjutant Helen Purviance fried doughnuts for American troops in France using soldiers' helmets as makeshift equipment, up to 9,000 a day. The "Doughnut Lassies" tie to soldier morale led the Salvation Army to create National Donut Day in 1938 in Chicago, a Depression-era fundraiser.
253ce0a6-01a4-4ed7-9a1a-f0727e6a528f	Frank Oz	The same performer voices and puppeteers both Yoda and Miss Piggy, and also did Cookie Monster, Bert, and Grover on Sesame Street.
26035a01-caff-4605-96f8-a8302db17b08	GUY (as in "guys")	After the 1605 Gunpowder Plot, crude straw effigies of Guy Fawkes ("guys") were burned every Nov. 5th. By 1836 "guy" meant any poorly/oddly dressed person in British English; by 1847 American English flattened it into a neutral word for any man at all.
26590e2a-3ef9-446a-9433-fac1b67b55bb	Operation Fortitude	Ahead of D-Day, the Allies built a fictional invasion force — the First United States Army Group — with inflatable rubber tanks, fake radio traffic, and dummy landing craft, publicly "commanded" by Gen. Patton, to convince Hitler the real invasion would hit Pas-de-Calais. Hitler held back Normandy reinforcements for seven weeks after D-Day.
26d67c8f-b1f3-4fe1-9f51-fa34aa4b77b7	AC/DC	After singer Bon Scott died of alcohol poisoning Feb. 1980, the band nearly disbanded — instead recruited Brian Johnson and recorded *Back in Black* seven weeks later (all-black cover as mourning). Sold ~50M copies, 2nd-best-selling album ever behind only *Thriller*; RIAA certified it 27x Platinum Aug. 21, 2024 — best-selling album in US history that never hit #1 on Billboard.
270c9a8f-277d-46a4-ba8b-211d07b9e3e9	MARIANA TRENCH	Challenger Deep plunges 10,935m (35,876 ft) — over 2km deeper than Everest is tall. In 2012, James Cameron (director of *Titanic* and *Avatar*) became the first person to solo-dive to the deepest point on the planet, alone in his submersible *Deepsea Challenger*.
2778a651-bcbb-40eb-a72a-c9025567c4a2	Cockpit	"Cockpit" started in 1587 as literally what it sounds like — a pit dug for cockfighting in Elizabethan England.
289192d5-4aed-44a5-94df-6a384c527972	The Haunted Mansion	The ride's iconic ghostly ballroom-dancer illusion is a live application of "Pepper's Ghost" — a stage trick popularized in 1862 by English scientist John Henry Pepper for a Christmas Eve production of a Dickens ghost story.
28b747bb-576b-4e28-84a9-7a345d732be0	Detroit Pistons	The Pistons weren't named for Detroit's auto industry — they arrived already named. Owner Fred Zollner ran the Zollner Piston Corporation, an auto-parts maker in Fort Wayne, Indiana, and named his 1941 team after his product. When he moved the franchise to Detroit in 1957, the name just happened to fit.
28cfb8cf-db71-44f0-a832-dce57f7f0e81	DOLLY THE SHEEP	Born 1996 at Scotland's Roslin Institute, the first mammal ever cloned from an adult cell rather than an embryo.
29029a1e-d04e-4d7f-acea-8efd7d3c9d61	Jumbo	The word "jumbo" for anything huge traces to ONE animal — Jumbo, the London Zoo African elephant P.T. Barnum bought in 1882 over massive public protest; jumbo jet, jumbo shrimp, and Jumbotron all descend from his name. Jumbo died in 1885 hit by a freight locomotive in St. Thomas, Ontario. Barnum donated the taxidermied hide to Tufts University, where it stood as mascot until an April 1975 fire destroyed Barnum Hall — the morning after, an employee swept Jumbo's ashes into an empty 14-oz Peter Pan crunchy peanut butter jar, which still sits in the Tufts athletic director's office, and athletes rub it for luck before games.
296aba76-b022-4acf-a67c-9344bda34f93	Better Made potato chips	Better Made potato chips have been made in Detroit since 1930, when Sicilian immigrants Cross Moceri and Peter Cipriano started frying chips in a garage after Moceri was denied a sales commission by another chip company. Nearly a century and 20-plus vanished local competitors later, the Cipriano family still owns it.
297b7d6a-83c8-46b4-a047-e5fd6c9ae949	Pluto	Pluto is the only planet (or, since 2006, dwarf planet) in the solar system named by an 11-year-old — Venetia Burney, an English schoolgirl, suggested the name to her grandfather over breakfast in 1930 after he read her the discovery news; the suggestion reached Lowell Observatory by telegram within days.
29e46860-3696-4a9e-9ac7-d1d9daa3c9e3	Immortal Jellyfish	Turritopsis dohrnii, discovered by a German biology student who accidentally left one in a jar too long in 1988, is the only known animal that can reverse its own aging — a stressed adult can revert to a juvenile polyp and restart its life cycle indefinitely.
2b58b1b8-1fe6-4556-aff9-42d7837f1489	THOR: RAGNAROK	Led Zeppelin's surviving members are famously stingy licensing their catalog to film — Taika Waititi got them to sign off on "Immigrant Song" only after starting talks extremely early and personally showing them the first trailer cut.
2bc4aa40-55bc-4879-8fd1-be98bfef04fa	University of Michigan	Apollo 15's crew, David Scott, Alfred Worden, and James Irwin, were all UMich alumni. Scott and Irwin left a 45-word plaque on the lunar surface reading "The University of Michigan Club of the Moon," making UMich the first and only school with a chartered alumni chapter on the moon.
2bd130d1-bc57-47d3-b686-47b74b178141	STATUE OF LIBERTY	Pre-dawn July 30, 1916, German agents blew up the Black Tom munitions depot in NY Harbor to stop US arms shipments to the Allies — a blast registering 5.5 on the Richter scale, felt 90 miles away in Philadelphia. Shrapnel struck the Statue of Liberty; its torch has been closed to visitors ever since.
2bf73b28-c40c-4a5d-8c8f-c22d45e63866	Sandra Bullock	In March 2010 she won Worst Actress at the Razzies (All About Steve) on Saturday and Best Actress at the Oscars (The Blind Side) on Sunday — the only performer ever to win both in the same weekend. She showed up to the Razzies in person towing a wagon loaded with All About Steve DVDs for the audience.
2bff2a19-6ac6-4199-b51e-c60af7dc17ed	THE NILE	One of the very few major rivers that flows north — empties into the Mediterranean after a ~7,000 km journey from sources near Lake Victoria (White Nile) and Lake Tana (Blue Nile), which merge at Khartoum.

2cdbabca-a527-4b4b-b2e5-4738c9f2d8fc	Bee Gees	The Bee Gees' "Stayin' Alive" (1977) runs at roughly 103 BPM — almost exactly the 100–120 BPM the American Heart Association recommends for CPR chest compressions — and the AHA now uses the song as an official training aid.

2e0d2596-40d7-4ec3-a20e-0b0db40494bf	GREAT BARRIER REEF	Made up of 2,900+ individual reefs and 900 islands stretching 2,300+ km — built entirely by billions of tiny coral polyps, the largest structure on Earth built by living organisms, visible from space.
2e6314b1-7d64-4a0f-b4ab-a5af75c69fa3	Neutron Star	A teaspoon of neutron star material would weigh about two billion tons on Earth — more than every human alive combined. In 2017, LIGO detected gravitational waves from two neutron stars colliding (GW170817); the resulting "kilonova" is now understood to be where most of the universe's gold comes from.
2e72d2b5-56c1-4164-9c18-47e80b6b2553	The Wizard of Oz	Terry, the Cairn Terrier who played Toto, earned $125 a week on the 1939 production — more than most of the human actors playing Munchkins, who made about $50 a week ($75 for speaking roles). Terry did her own stunts and even broke a leg on set after a Winkie guard's costume boot stepped on her.
2eaf6ef8-a417-476e-aee7-e82de3be944f	7 Up (original lithium citrate formula)	7 Up's original 1929 formula contained lithium citrate — a genuine psychiatric mood stabilizer still used today to treat bipolar disorder — and was marketed as a hangover cure and mood lifter. The FDA banned lithium from soft drinks in 1948, forcing a reformulation two years later.
2ed6f39b-fc28-42d6-8ed9-ed0949323f54	Big Thunder Mountain Railroad	An MSU urologist, David Wartinger, 3D-printed a model kidney and rode Big Thunder Mountain Railroad at Walt Disney World 20 times to test whether the ride helps pass kidney stones — the study found a ~70% dislodge success rate on that specific coaster (Space Mountain and Rock 'n' Roller Coaster failed).
2f243996-d38f-407c-aeb3-972f0e4a0b4a	The road centerline (Edward Hines, Michigan)	The painted centerline dividing traffic lanes was first laid down in 1911 by Edward Hines of the Wayne County (Michigan) Road Board, and is often called the single most important traffic-safety device ever devised. The charming story that a leaky milk wagon dripping a white trail gave him the idea is probably apocryphal.

2f34daeb-3618-40d1-b2cb-1f8b9f7abc8f	TARDIGRADE	In a 2007 ESA experiment (FOTON-M3/TARDIS), dehydrated tardigrades were exposed to the open vacuum of space and solar UV for 10 days — some survived, the first known animal to survive raw space exposure.
2f3f89fd-d60d-4bc4-b7a5-e8c92bd00133	"BLAZE OF GLORY" (Bon Jovi)	Jon Bon Jovi wrote it on a diner napkin after borrowing the *Young Guns II* script from star Emilio Estevez. Hit #1, Oscar-nominated for Best Original Song (lost to Madonna's "Sooner or Later"), won the Golden Globe.
2f842930-d00d-493c-ad9e-4b06f6cdc996	Andrew Jackson	In 1836 dairymen from Oswego County, NY shipped Jackson a 1,400-pound cheddar wheel — 4 feet across, hauled by 24 horses. He let it age in the White House Entrance Hall for over a year, then on February 22, 1837 (Washington's Birthday), weeks before leaving office, invited the public in; the whole wheel was devoured in about two hours. Successor Van Buren had to replace the drapes and repaint to kill the smell.
2f88daee-5e94-406e-97cc-90952e6ac5e4	Vernors	Vernors is the oldest surviving ginger ale in the United States, first served in Detroit in 1866 by James Vernor — Michigan's first registered pharmacist. Company legend says he aged the syrup in an oak cask while serving four years in the Civil War and came home to find it improved.
30910959-32ab-484e-b97d-854a3538747a	COBALT	From German *kobold* — a household goblin. Harz Mountains miners in the 1600s named the ore after the mischievous spirit because it looked like valuable ore but yielded nothing and poisoned them with arsenic fumes when smelted.
30ac2564-56b9-410d-95ab-fd1b9634215d	Thanos	Marvel writer-artist Jim Starlin created Thanos in 1973 (Iron Man #55) after a college psychology course introduced him to "Thanatos" — the personification of the death drive in psychoanalytic theory. Starlin has said in interviews he believed he was naming the character after a Freud concept; the death instinct was actually coined by Freud's colleague Wilhelm Stekel in 1909, not Freud himself.
3112911e-cd99-4b2a-ac14-fab41423ee7f	ROBOT	Czech writer Karel Čapek introduced "robot" to English in his 1920 play R.U.R. (premiered Jan 2, 1921), from Czech "robota" (forced labor) — Čapek credited his brother Josef with actually coining the word.
31366885-3afd-49c7-ab64-74b90652ceb6	Escalator	'Escalator' was once a brand name. Charles Seeberger coined it around 1900 from the Latin 'scala' (steps) plus 'elevator,' trademarked it, and sold the rights to Otis. But Otis used the word so loosely in its own advertising that a 1950 court ruled it had gone generic — the company lost the trademark by making it too famous.
3219f977-c8ce-4c79-af2f-6c3156625087	Denmark	From 1984 to 2022 Canada and Denmark fought the "Whisky War" over Hans Island, a 1.2 km² barren rock between Ellesmere Island and Greenland — each side's military would land, swap the flag, and leave the other a bottle (Danish schnapps/Gammel Dansk vs Canadian whisky). The June 14, 2022 treaty split the island along a natural ridge (~60% to Denmark), giving Canada its first land border with any country other than the US — a 1,280-meter line that is the northernmost international land border on Earth. The foreign ministers exchanged bottles at the signing.
326f2f6d-9feb-4572-bd73-c6cac3e0c392	Wiffle ball	The Wiffle ball's eight oblong holes exist so a 12-year-old could throw a curveball without straining his arm. David N. Mullany designed it in his Fairfield, Connecticut backyard in 1953 after his son complained that pitching a perforated plastic golf ball made his arm ache; the name comes from kids calling a strikeout a 'whiff.'
32da5583-d4d7-47a1-ab33-63613ffe1308	Bo Jackson	On March 25, 1986, Buccaneers owner Hugh Culverhouse flew him to Tampa on his private jet for a physical under assurance the trip was NCAA-cleared — it wasn't, and it voided the rest of his Auburn baseball season. Convinced it was sabotage to kill his baseball option, he warned the Bucs "You draft me if you want. You're going to waste a draft pick." They took him #1 overall anyway; he signed with the Kansas City Royals instead.
32f2b133-991a-4f7c-8965-d33da19709de	Gargantuan	"Gargantuan" comes from Gargantua, the enormous food-devouring giant in François Rabelais's 16th-century French novels — entering English in the 1590s.
3387d880-9e8a-441f-b87b-ab56e3a93054	Doritos (Casa de Fritos, Disneyland)	Doritos started as an anti-waste fix at a Disneyland restaurant. In the early 1960s, a cook at Casa de Fritos inside the park was told to stop throwing out stale tortillas, so he cut them into triangles, fried them, and gave them away free. A Frito-Lay executive noticed how popular the giveaway snack had become and took it national by 1966.
33b28cfa-4fcb-4b09-aad6-e58e61eae8de	Ozzy Osbourne	On January 20, 1982, at Des Moines' Veterans Memorial Auditorium, a teenage fan threw a bat onstage and Ozzy Osbourne bit its head off, thinking it was a rubber toy. It wasn't — he spent the rest of that tour getting nightly rabies-preventive shots in both arms, both thighs, and both cheeks. The stunt became a lightning rod for the religious right's 1980s crusade against heavy metal.
33c8390b-cc96-4465-b8bd-3122f18c9d29	WOODPECKER	A woodpecker's skull absorbs ~1,200 g's of force per peck, up to 20x/second, without concussion. UC Berkeley researchers reverse-engineered the mechanism into a shock absorber protecting test contents at 60,000 g's — proposed for flight-recorder protection (vs. ~1,000 g's a black box is built for).
33fd5913-9789-4726-9a32-4a7485aa7ec6	Twisted Sister	On September 19, 1985, Twisted Sister frontman Dee Snider testified before a U.S. Senate committee alongside Frank Zappa and John Denver, defending his band's lyrics against the Parents Music Resource Center's obscenity claims. He personally rebutted Tipper Gore's claim that "Under the Blade" promoted sadomasochism, explaining the song was actually about the fear of surgery. The hearing led directly to the "Parental Advisory" sticker still printed on albums today.
34158e29-8bfb-4d92-9fd5-2399a22eb75d	John Denver	At the 1975 CMA Awards, presenter Charlie Rich — the reigning Entertainer of the Year — tore open the envelope, pulled out a cigarette lighter, and set the winner's card ON FIRE on live TV before announcing "my friend, Mr. John Denver." Nashville read it as a protest against pop crossover; Rich's son says he was on pain meds for a broken foot plus dressing-room gin and tonics. Rich's career never recovered — no Top 10 hit again until 1977.
3428f45a-be04-43b4-bcf8-c6f8f72f563e	Segway (Dean Kamen / Jimi Heselden)	MYTH-BUST: The Segway inventor did NOT die on a Segway. Dean Kamen (the inventor) is alive; Jimi Heselden, the businessman who BOUGHT the company, drove one off a cliff on his estate in 2010. The garbled version circulates constantly in fact lists.
343753f4-1bef-494d-afc7-3b33e45b2aad	R.E.M.	Michael Stipe picked the name R.E.M. completely at random out of a dictionary in 1980, after the band nearly went with "Cans of Piss" or "Negro Eyes." Everyone assumes it stands for rapid eye movement — the dream stage of sleep — but when Dr. William Dement, the actual sleep scientist who coined the term "REM sleep," personally reached out to the band, they told him it wasn't named after REM sleep at all. Pure coincidence — one Dement found funny enough to put the band's photo in his own lectures.
344f80a8-449a-4a5a-833c-1361bb05da94	Twinkie	There's no such thing, legally, as the "Twinkie defense." At Dan White's 1979 trial for killing San Francisco Mayor George Moscone and Supervisor Harvey Milk, his lawyers argued longstanding, untreated depression diminished his capacity for premeditation — a psychiatrist cited White's sudden switch to junk food as a symptom of that depression, not a cause of murder. The press invented the "Twinkie defense" label; the term itself was never used in court. Fallout from the case was real, though: California abolished the "diminished capacity" defense entirely in 1982.
34ccc48b-e1ae-439b-864e-0be9b6ff2893	The designated hitter	The National League voted on adopting the DH in August 1980 — and the measure died 4–5 with 3 abstentions because Phillies owner Ruly Carpenter was unreachable on a family fishing trip, so VP Bill Giles, unsure how his boss wanted to vote once the start date slipped to 1982, had to abstain. The NL then went another 42 years without the DH, finally adopting it in 2022. Carpenter later read from his fishing log: that day he caught "six flounder, 12 weakfish, and two croakers."
354a040f-271f-4b1a-a568-4866dd1567db	Adobe	The software company Adobe is named after Adobe Creek, a small stream that ran behind co-founder John Warnock's home in Los Altos, California.
35dc471c-bb69-49a9-9778-98359351caff	Mata Hari	Dutch exotic dancer Margaretha Zelle performed as "Mata Hari" (Malay for "eye of the day," the sun). Convicted by French military tribunal of WW1 espionage on thin, largely circumstantial evidence, she was executed by firing squad at Vincennes on October 15, 1917.
367b3ee1-be7c-4668-ace2-206cce1b6e49	CLEVELAND GUARDIANS	The Indians rebranded to Guardians in 2022, named for the "Guardians of Traffic" — eight 1932 Art Deco pylon sculptures on the Hope Memorial Bridge next to the ballpark.
368448ae-9396-4fa0-8eec-25519f583fd9	AMELIA EARHART	Her July 1937 Pacific disappearance triggered a US Navy/Coast Guard search costing ~$4 million — the most expensive air-and-sea search in American history to that point — finding nothing.
368b222c-750c-48ee-ad3a-07b0c3f1764f	Oprah Winfrey	Oprah's hometown — Kosciusko, Mississippi (b. 1954, on her grandmother's farm) — and Australia's highest peak, Mount Kosciuszko (7,310 ft, named in 1840 by Polish explorer Paweł Strzelecki), are both named for the same man: Tadeusz Kościuszko, the Polish general of the American Revolution who engineered the fortifications at West Point. Kościuszko never set foot in Australia — he'd been dead 23 years when the mountain got his name.
372fcc18-151f-4eb0-93f5-9ee0c03e8993	DIANA ROSS	Born in Detroit (1944), lead singer of the Supremes, Motown's biggest act, per the Rock & Roll Hall of Fame popular enough to "rival even the Beatles."
373236f5-a499-4bac-998e-be8250538548	Survivor ("Eye of the Tiger")	Sylvester Stallone wanted a new song for Rocky III in 1982 and reportedly asked Queen for permission to use "Another One Bites the Dust" — they turned him down. He called up Survivor guitarist Frankie Sullivan instead, and Sullivan and keyboardist Jim Peterik wrote "Eye of the Tiger" on commission as the film's theme. It spent six weeks at No. 1 on the Billboard Hot 100 that summer and won the Grammy for Best Rock Performance by a Duo or Group.

3882c15b-1bc2-4e0f-828d-e67bce041fb6	Teflon	DuPont chemist Roy Plunkett discovered Teflon (PTFE) completely by accident on April 6, 1938, while testing a refrigerant gas — a supposedly empty cylinder was lined with a strange, slippery white polymer.
389434e4-c44e-4f4a-88ea-72f5df881ecf	Stockard Channing	She was 33 years old playing high-school senior Rizzo in Grease, thirteen years past the character's supposed age, and later joked she took the job because "the mortgage was whispering in my ear."
38a6d8af-372d-41da-9a5b-9d8248b1e3ab	VEGAS GOLDEN KNIGHTS	In their 2017-18 inaugural season, won the Pacific Division and reached the Stanley Cup Final — the first true expansion team in any of the 4 major US leagues to win its division in year one.
38b6113e-cc6f-4af0-81be-ee452243e619	Saran Wrap	Saran Wrap started as an accident in a Dow Chemical lab in Midland, Michigan in 1933, when researcher Ralph Wiley couldn't get a sticky film out of a beaker. Dow's first use wasn't food storage — the military sprayed it on fighter planes to protect against salty sea spray during WWII, and it went into car upholstery, before Dow sold it as kitchen wrap starting in 1949 (household version 1953).
39b4e53e-0aa9-4f52-bc46-3a6f7e9e9ae0	The Principality of Sealand	A retired British anti-aircraft sea fort 7 miles off the English coast has been claimed as an independent micronation since 1967, when a British family seized it from pirate radio broadcasters and declared it sovereign — complete with its own flag, currency, and passports, and a defense record that includes repelling both rival broadcasters and the Royal Navy with gunfire.
39c81f32-0bf3-42b5-99f7-7a921cc06d18	MALCOLM X	Family fled Omaha for Lansing, Michigan after KKK threats against his father; the home was later targeted by the white-supremacist Black Legion, and his father died under disputed circumstances there in 1931 when Malcolm was six.
3a005254-fc06-4adb-8943-9f8f99c55109	555	Phone companies pushed Hollywood to fake phone numbers with the 555 prefix starting in the early 1960s to stop pranksters dialing real numbers. Bruce Almighty ignored the rule in 2003 and gave "God's" phone number as a real, working number, flooding real people with calls before the studio swapped it for 555-0123 on DVD.
3a72f162-b633-41fd-9cee-9cc7c4bbd7bf	John Lasseter	Before founding Pixar, he helped build what's considered the first fully computer-generated character ever in a feature film, a knight stepping out of a stained-glass window in the largely-forgotten 1985 flop Young Sherlock Holmes.
3aa391e5-c7e5-40a6-9cc2-007aa6409355	Magic: The Gathering	Magic: The Gathering, released August 5, 1993, is Guinness World Records' official holder for "first modern trading card game" — Wizards of the Coast shipped 2.5 million cards to that year's Gen Con and sold out in weeks. It was inducted into the National Toy Hall of Fame in 2019.
3b1d183a-70de-40bb-88fc-5a207edf841c	Lyin' Eyes	In a 2008 Dublin murder-for-hire trial, the prosecutor quoted "you can't hide those lyin' eyes" in closing arguments - Sharon Collins had used the email "lyingeyes98@yahoo.ie" to hire a hitman off a website, and claimed she didn't know it was an Eagles lyric until after her arrest.
3b3ca4fd-866f-42f8-bcc1-8461f0438107	Detroit-style pizza	Detroit-style pizza was born in 1946 at Buddy's Rendezvous, a former speakeasy, when Gus Guerra baked a Sicilian-style pie in a blue-steel tray borrowed from the auto industry — pans originally made to hold small factory parts or catch drips under machinery. The tall steel sides are why the cheese caramelizes into those signature crispy edges.
3b3db78d-698d-4150-8e30-98199068c5da	CHARLES LINDBERGH	The 1932 kidnapping/murder of his 20-month-old son, "the Crime of the Century," directly pushed Congress to pass the Federal Kidnapping Act — still known as "the Lindbergh Law."
3be63459-7600-4534-a57e-95c3f092bef0	Whitney Houston	"I Will Always Love You" (The Bodyguard, 1992) was written by Dolly Parton in 1973 — Houston's version is Guinness-certified as the best-selling physical single by a female solo artist, at 24 million copies.
3c5f9c51-a362-4087-b4fb-e2a36026bd53	Point Nemo	The single most remote spot on Earth's oceans sits 1,670 miles from the nearest land in any direction — so isolated that its closest human neighbors, when the ISS passes overhead, are literally astronauts in orbit 250 miles up. Space agencies exploit that emptiness deliberately: it's the designated crash site for dead satellites and space stations, over 250 of them so far, including Russia's Mir.
3ccf59fa-9a4d-477f-bedf-2c328253eac6	The Godfather	The severed horse's head in the 1972 film was real — Coppola's crew found a horse already destined for slaughter at a New Jersey dog-food plant and had the head shipped to the set packed in dry ice, rather than kill an animal for the film. The American Humane Association, which monitored the shoot, approved the scene at the time since the horse's death wasn't caused by the production; that approval was criticized by the organization's own later leadership.

3e16316e-9368-41e1-9c04-bcc9501d0193	Eddie Gaedel	The shortest player in MLB history was Eddie Gaedel, 3-foot-7 and 65 pounds, sent up to bat by showman owner Bill Veeck for the 1951 St. Louis Browns wearing number '1/8.' Pitchers couldn't find his tiny strike zone, so he walked on four pitches; the league voided the stunt the next day, leaving his career on-base percentage a perfect 1.000.
3e276202-c24d-43fd-b04a-9f36eca19e4e	The Kraken	Carl Linnaeus — the father of scientific classification — listed the Kraken as a real animal in the first edition of Systema Naturae (1735), classifying it as a cephalopod under the name Microcosmus marinus. He quietly dropped it from later editions, though his 1746 Fauna Suecica still described it as "a unique monster" in Norwegian seas (with a disclaimer that he'd never seen one).
3e39b24f-4e72-4ad7-8127-354970200de2	Somewhere in Time	The 1980 Christopher Reeve time-travel romance was filmed almost entirely at the Grand Hotel on Mackinac Island, Michigan — an island that has banned motor vehicles since an 1898 ordinance passed after cars kept spooking carriage horses.
3ec43363-de4b-4c21-adc9-c6c4fc1b3b13	NICOTINE	Named for Jean Nicot, France's ambassador to Portugal, who shipped tobacco seeds to Paris in 1561 and gave leaves to Catherine de Medici as a migraine cure.
3ec94b9e-d136-4d8d-a6c1-89a8b1e83d5b	RADIOHEAD	Before Radiohead, the Oxford band was "On a Friday." EMI wanted a new name, so they lifted "Radiohead" from Talking Heads' 1986 song "Radio Head" (album *True Stories*, written for David Byrne's film of the same name).
3f148fc0-0b6d-4d8d-a7f0-b129419f6b59	Psycho	The blood in the 1960 shower scene was Hershey's chocolate syrup, chosen because it read more convincingly than stage blood on black-and-white film stock. Hitchcock originally wanted the scene to run with no music at all; composer Bernard Herrmann wrote the now-famous shrieking string cue anyway, Hitchcock heard it and reversed himself on the spot, later saying "33% of the effect of Psycho was due to the music."
3f2bcdb9-b78e-4be8-9da0-5f247457dd5a	Cadillac	Detroit folklore says the city's French founder Antoine de la Mothe Cadillac was warned by a fortune teller about a demon called the Nain Rouge but struck it with his cane and cursed it instead; his fortune collapsed soon after. Wikipedia notes no record of the legend exists before the 1880s, nearly 180 years after his supposed 1701 encounter.
3ff060d3-e473-4d18-af0d-51ede8667c6d	The Slinky	The Slinky exists because a naval engineer, Richard James, was trying to design torsion springs to keep sensitive ship instruments steady in rough seas at a Philadelphia shipyard in 1943. He knocked one off a shelf, watched it 'walk' end over end instead of just falling, and had a toy company within a year. His wife Betty found the name in a dictionary.
41f52331-52b1-4ef8-9e03-a3d4ecc183f4	KENTUCKY DERBY	The "garland of roses" tradition began 1896 (554 red roses on green satin), inspired by socialite E. Berry Wall handing out roses at an 1883 post-Derby party. "Run for the Roses" nickname coined by sportswriter Bill Corum in 1925.
42a31d9c-dad7-477a-827e-50324bb8f7b7	TRIVIA	From Latin *trivia*, plural of *trivium* — "the place where three roads meet" (*tri-* + *via*). Romans also called this a *trivius dea*, "goddess of three ways," another name for Hecate, patron of crossroads.

439272ec-c1e4-41f3-b3d9-b726516b911a	PONTIAC	In May 1763, Odawa war chief Pontiac led a tribal coalition in a months-long siege of British-held Fort Detroit, briefly seizing 9 of 11 British forts in the Ohio Valley (Pontiac's War). Made peace in 1766, assassinated 1769. GM named its Pontiac auto brand after the Michigan city bearing his name (1926-2010).
43e11a40-0456-46e2-b22c-d13c5b09c9e3	Back to the Future	Eric Stoltz was the original Marty McFly and shot roughly five weeks of footage before director Robert Zemeckis fired him for playing the role too seriously and recast Michael J. Fox, adding about $3 million to the budget for reshoots. 443cdb5c-403c-499e-9793-1c17b7da2f2b	John Ratzenberger	In January 1993 he and George Wendt sued Host International over animatronic robot versions of Cliff and Norm drinking in Cheers-replica airport bars Host had licensed from Paramount — Paramount owned the characters, but the actors owned their faces. The right-of-publicity fight (Wendt v. Host International) ran eight years, reached the Supreme Court (which turned Paramount away in 2000), and settled in 2001.
4442cdbc-298e-4e6f-809e-770f5dafb7fb	Life Savers	MYTH-BUST: Life Savers were not invented after a candy maker's child choked to death on hard candy — that popular tearjerker is false. In 1912, Ohio candy maker Clarence Crane simply borrowed a pill-stamping machine from a pharmacy to punch a hole in his mints so they wouldn't melt in summer like his chocolates did, and named them for their life-preserver shape.
44b32861-dd32-4cbd-afb8-42ebca56e8fa	Little Brown Jug	College football's Michigan-Minnesota trophy started as a paranoid mistake: worried Minnesota might tamper with the water supply before their unbeaten 1903 showdown, Michigan coach Fielding Yost bought a 30-cent water jug — then left it behind. Minnesota's equipment man found it, painted the 6-6 final score on it, and the schools turned it into a traveling trophy in 1909.
44e5a1b3-4406-42f7-890e-b418dd939ac2	PETRA	Petra's Treasury facade, carved by the Nabataeans 2,000+ years ago, played the Holy Grail temple in *Indiana Jones and the Last Crusade*. In 2024, archaeologists digging beneath it found a real intact 2,000-year-old tomb with at least 12 skeletons.
454d86bd-d8b9-4a2a-b419-e1192c0128c1	LAIKA	Nov. 3, 1957, the USSR launched stray dog Laika aboard Sputnik 2 — first animal to orbit Earth. Soviet officials claimed she survived days; she actually died within hours from cabin overheating.
46e3a5ac-9770-4190-8d0f-10352f30d27f	THE SIXTH SENSE	M. Night Shyamalan deliberately kept the color red almost entirely out of the film except in moments tied to the supernatural (a doorknob, a tent, a balloon, a shirt).
47eedf99-5ef1-419d-92d2-38e12827d36c	The Heimlich maneuver (Henry Heimlich)	Dr. Henry Heimlich published his namesake choking-rescue technique in 1974 — then, by his own account, didn't personally perform it on a real choking victim until he was 96 years old, in his own retirement community. His son Peter has publicly disputed both of his father's claimed 'first use' stories as fabricated.
484ea88a-3223-490e-9252-e6d716b0bc85	Se7en	A San Francisco photographer named Jorge Sandoval sued the makers of Se7en, claiming ten of his own copyrighted self-portraits were used without permission as the unsettling photos pinned to the serial killer's apartment wall. 48b71566-d764-47be-978b-6e9ba76e6dbe	Spirit in the Sky	The fuzz-rock gospel anthem about dying with Jesus was written by Norman Greenbaum — an Orthodox-raised Jewish one-hit wonder — in about 15 minutes after he saw Porter Wagoner sing a gospel number on TV and thought "I could do that." 49bc39f4-3e6f-4b9e-8be3-3d9cd96cf593	Zebra	In 2025 a team of Japanese researchers won the Ig Nobel Prize in Biology for painting black cattle with white, zebra-style stripes — the pattern cut biting-fly landings by up to 50%, building on a 2014 study of why zebras evolved stripes in the first place.
49d5249d-26c3-431f-b7ee-a2a133f83a72	Arthur Conan Doyle	The creator of literature's greatest rationalist was completely fooled by two schoolgirls. In 1917, cousins Elsie Wright (16) and Frances Griffiths (9) photographed "fairies" in Cottingley, England — paper cutouts traced from a 1914 children's book (Princess Mary's Gift Book) and propped up with hatpins. Doyle publicly endorsed the photos and wrote a whole book about them, The Coming of the Fairies (1922). The cousins didn't confess until 1983 — 66 years later.
4ada0f0c-fbd9-4cca-b12f-37160cafdf30	TREVOR HOFFMAN	Starting July 25, 1998, Hoffman entered Padres save situations to AC/DC's "Hells Bells" with a tolling-bell scoreboard animation ("Trevor Time") — so popular other teams requested tape of it.
4bd9e2a5-04ff-4373-b0d7-ae645b49b0a4	Silly Putty	Silly Putty was a WWII failure. A GE engineer, James Wright, was trying to make a cheap synthetic rubber and instead got a bouncing goo that copied newsprint but was useless for tires. A marketer, Peter Hodgson, later packaged it in plastic Easter eggs in 1950 and sold a quarter-million in three days.
4c61406f-2770-40fe-87d3-1d315fac7b04	Kit Kat	In Japan, Kit Kats are exam-season good luck charms because 'Kitto Katto' sounds like 'kitto katsu' — 'you will surely win.' Roughly one in three Japanese students buys them before entrance exams, and Nestlé prints encouragement messages on special exam editions.
4c776b13-e615-4e23-a2e0-bbe85a0aca1f	Annie Oakley	In 1903 Hearst's Chicago papers reported Annie Oakley had been jailed for stealing a man's trousers to fund a cocaine habit — the arrestee was actually a burlesque performer working under the stage name "Any Oakley." Oakley spent the next six years suing all 55 papers that ran the story, one by one, in one of the largest libel actions in US history — and won or settled all but one. Hearst even hired an investigator to dig up dirt on her; he found nothing.
4d33d955-2b93-4cfb-bbd7-24c4192ee424	Susan Sarandon	The Oscar winner (Dead Man Walking) voiced Granny Rags — a blind, deranged, black-magic street witch — in the 2012 video game Dishonored, in a cast that also hid Carrie Fisher (as the government propaganda loudspeakers), Lena Headey, Michael Madsen, Brad Dourif and John Slattery. 4de1a0d7-a92e-439f-81e9-a89ce534b48d	the Netherlands	In January 1943, Canada's government declared one maternity ward of Ottawa Civic Hospital temporarily extraterritorial so that Princess Margriet — born there while the Dutch royal family sat out the Nazi occupation in exile — would be born solely a Dutch citizen, not a British subject. The day she was born, the Dutch tricolour flew from the Peace Tower — the only foreign flag ever flown over Canada's Parliament. The oft-told version that the ward "became Dutch soil" is false: it was made no-man's-land, not Dutch territory.
4de5d2b7-991f-4c98-a9de-9ab7fe4f4ea4	Frisbee	The Frisbee is named after the Frisbie Pie Company of Bridgeport, Connecticut — Yale students flung the empty pie tins and yelled 'Frisbie!' as a warning. When Wham-O bought Fred Morrison's 'Pluto Platter' flying disc in 1957, they took the name and changed one letter to dodge a trademark fight.
4e0a0c13-c0ee-4736-bd27-53ac538f8cc7	GALÁPAGOS ISLANDS	Named for their giant tortoises — "galápago" is an old Spanish/pre-Roman word for tortoise. Charles Darwin visited in 1835 aboard HMS Beagle, observations that fed directly into his theory of evolution.

51c7515a-f123-4b72-89d4-a244a5a21d38	DEATH VALLEY	Holds the hottest reliably-measured temp on Earth — 130°F at Furnace Creek (Aug 16 2020, July 9 2021); the older 134°F/1913 WMO record is now publicly disputed by meteorologists as ~14°F too high. Also holds the opposite extreme: Badwater Basin sits 282 ft below sea level (lowest point in North America), 15 miles from Telescope Peak (11,043 ft) — the steepest high-low swing over the shortest distance in the Lower 48.

527e22e2-cf8c-459a-962f-4a0c43548719	St. Valentine's Day Massacre	The Tommy guns used in the 1929 Chicago massacre were only recovered because gangster Fred "Killer" Burke fled to Michigan, killed a cop in a car-crash aftermath in St. Joseph, and a raid on his house there turned up the weapons - matching them via rifling marks became one of the first major uses of forensic ballistics in US history.
