5328a0a3-55cb-49d5-a149-935f3b3ae8d0	The Rocky Horror Picture Show	It flopped on opening (Sept 26, 1975) and Fox withdrew it everywhere but LA — then relaunched it as a midnight movie at Greenwich Village's Waverly Theater on April Fools' Day 1976, and because Fox never formally pulled the title, it is technically still on its original 1975 release: the longest-running theatrical release in film history, 50 years and counting. (Prominence note: the record sits in the Wikipedia lead — the never-pulled/April Fools' mechanics are the deep layer to build on.)
53674704-be99-4eb3-927e-496ff9ff5e28	The White House	Its entire interior is a 1950s rebuild. In summer 1948 a leg of Margaret Truman's piano broke through the second floor; engineers found split beams and declared the building "standing only from force of habit." From late 1948 to March 1952 it was gutted to its outer sandstone shell — bulldozers drove around inside — and rebuilt on a steel frame for $5.4M while the Trumans lived across the street at Blair House.
5570e889-97e3-46ba-8a22-3a88e501bc39	Renegade	Barack Obama's Secret Service code name is "Renegade," officially assigned by the White House Communications Agency in November 2008. Per tradition, the rest of his immediate family got names starting with the same letter: Michelle is "Renaissance," daughters Malia and Sasha are "Radiance" and "Rosebud."
561665f0-ac78-4427-a658-2ee2978a9fd4	WAYNE GRETZKY	As a junior player for the Sault Ste. Marie Greyhounds, Gretzky wanted No. 9 to honor Gordie Howe — taken, so his coach suggested doubling it: 99. The NHL retired 99 league-wide Feb. 6, 2000 — the only number ever retired across every NHL team, explicitly modeled on MLB retiring Jackie Robinson's 42 league-wide in 1997.
567ab01b-f422-4111-b9dd-bf2452748fec	MARIE CURIE	Still the only person in history to win Nobel Prizes in two different scientific fields — Physics (1903) and Chemistry (1911).
569e48f7-0012-4037-9640-e2615edea2e9	Neptune	Deep inside Neptune (and Uranus), scientists believe the pressure is so extreme that methane gets crushed apart and its carbon atoms reassemble into actual diamonds, which then rain down through the planet's interior. In 2017, SLAC's X-ray laser recreated those exact pressure-and-heat conditions on ordinary plastic in a lab and filmed diamonds forming in real time, confirming the theory.
56c3fd68-c8be-4352-b5cf-dae2335cb1ad	Devils Tower	On September 24, 1906, Theodore Roosevelt used the brand-new Antiquities Act to name this 1,267-foot Wyoming volcanic formation the very first U.S. National Monument — before the National Park Service even existed (est. 1916).
56d40772-8b0a-423a-8843-e9b61a6c50ea	Elmo	On April 23, 2002, Elmo testified before the House Appropriations Subcommittee on Labor, HHS and Education — in a tiny suit, lobbying for school music-education funding — the first Muppet ever to appear in an official capacity before Congress. Per the House's own historical archives, he was also the first congressional witness to try to eat the microphone.
5721ffc0-ae14-4dea-9bab-7544f11af8d8	GREENLAND SHARK	Longest-living vertebrate known to science — estimated 250 to 500+ years old, via radiocarbon-dating eye-lens proteins. Doesn't reach sexual maturity until ~age 150.
58f24f15-2405-44a2-8515-0381b23b0f6b	Monaco	At just under 0.8 square miles, Monaco is the second-smallest sovereign country on Earth — smaller than New York's Central Park, behind only Vatican City.
5a39d5eb-1ece-48c0-88e7-e78a751018e1	ZZ Top	Drummer Frank Beard is the only member of ZZ Top without a beard — Billy Gibbons and Dusty Hill grew their trademark beards during a mid-1970s hiatus and never shaved again.
5a53f905-44fe-4705-8367-d9dd44e3d7fc	NICKELBACK	Bassist Mike Kroeger coined the name working a Vancouver Starbucks register — $1.95 coffee, customers paying with $2, him saying "here's your nickel back." Prior band name: Village Idiot.
5aca581c-876e-4ab8-938e-4a4a5f82302e	Bewitched	Darrin Stephens was recast mid-run — Dick York, sidelined by a degenerative spinal injury and painkiller dependency, collapsed on set in 1968 and was replaced for the final three seasons by Dick Sargent, who had actually been the producers' first choice back in 1964.
5b061381-aa36-4d6d-ac60-03ca55e6afa6	Dungeons & Dragons	D&D is the only traditional tabletop role-playing game ever inducted into the National Toy Hall of Fame (2016) — it beat out Clue, Uno, and Rock 'Em Sock 'Em Robots that year, and no other RPG has joined the Hall since.
5b2806d6-5441-43db-8617-5d716e56ddac	Cracker Jack	Cracker Jack started putting a 'Prize in Every Box' in 1912 and had handed out roughly 17 billion toys by the time Frito-Lay finally pulled physical prizes in 2016, replacing them with a scan-code sticker for a digital game instead.
5b90d6d0-9d4c-4e9b-ac48-3b887ed22a60	YAHTZEE	Invented by an anonymous Canadian couple who played it on their yacht ("The Yacht Game") and approached entrepreneur Edwin S. Lowe to manufacture sets as gifts — Lowe trademarked "Yahtzee" in 1956.
5bbbe26b-bbca-44d1-95e7-94cd4c58b23b	FIG NEWTON	Not named for Sir Isaac Newton — Kennedy Biscuit Company plant manager James Hazen named it in 1891 after Newton, Massachusetts, the Boston suburb where it was made.
5be764cf-82dd-4df3-bf4e-d2975cf0ce89	Danny Trejo	Robert Rodriguez first wrote "Machete" for Danny Trejo in the '90s, then quietly slipped him into the family-friendly Spy Kids trilogy as the kids' gadget-dealing uncle, nearly a decade before spinning him off into the ultra-violent Machete franchise.
5c87762f-3e50-4b7a-9b89-4679079b8823	Lord	"Lord" comes from Old English "hlafweard" — literally "loaf-ward," the guardian of the household's bread.
5cc485c7-b582-479a-94f5-6b7c5e014110	Milburn Pennybags	Monopoly's mascot wasn't officially named "Mr. Monopoly" until a 1999 Hasbro rebrand — for decades before that he was "Rich Uncle Pennybags," full name Milburn Pennybags, first named in a 1946 Parker Brothers game called Rich Uncle.
5d889ff5-2d9e-4599-83ab-ef7a28801dc9	NIKE	Nike Inc. renamed itself in 1971 after the Greek goddess of victory. That same year, Portland State design student Carolyn Davidson designed the "swoosh" and was paid just $35 by co-founder Phil Knight.
5dc89953-4059-4972-b8b5-894095f6bab0	Barenaked Ladies	The name was a total accident. Ed Robertson's cover band fell apart right before a battle-of-the-bands slot; to keep the booking, he improvised on the phone that his "new" band was called Barenaked Ladies — a nonsense name he and Steven Page used to invent together for fun. It stuck. Years later, Robertson wrote CBS's "The Big Bang Theory" theme song in about 15 minutes in the shower, after show creators Chuck Lorre and Bill Prady happened to catch him freestyle-rapping about the actual Big Bang at a BNL concert.
5f208a53-94be-487d-b73e-3f4b53a00413	99 Luftballons	Nena's guitarist Carlo Karges got the idea at a 1982 Rolling Stones concert in West Berlin, when the band released a stack of balloons into the sky — he watched one drift toward East Berlin and imagined a Cold War radar operator mistaking it for an incoming threat. He wrote the lyrics that same night.
5f720c28-cc37-4fd2-9846-57ed82f9e5b6	Mario Lemieux	On December 31, 1988, Mario Lemieux scored five goals in one game in all five legal ways: even strength, power play, shorthanded, on a penalty shot, and into an empty net. No NHL player has ever repeated it, and fans later voted it the greatest moment in league history.
5fddf7dd-e9b6-4681-b3af-7c4d209f73f1	FROZEN	Rapunzel and Flynn Rider from *Tangled* appear as background wedding guests in Elsa's coronation crowd — a deliberate Disney crossover cameo.
60169d0f-df9a-4252-9e73-f24743c2bf22	CAP'N CRUNCH	His original voice (1963-1988) was Daws Butler, the same actor behind Huckleberry Hound and Quick Draw McGraw — based on 1930s character actor Charles Butterworth.
6021906c-904a-44a3-a743-f7d66496ed5b	Def Leppard	Def Leppard started life as "Deaf Leopard" — a name Joe Elliott dreamed up in English class. His bandmates hated being named after a hearing-impaired animal, so drummer Tony Kenning suggested swapping the spelling. Nobody noticed for about a month that the new spelling now echoed Led Zeppelin — and once they did, they decided they'd rather look like Zeppelin than the alternative they were about to pick, The Flying Lizards.
604d3b28-93a7-4f3a-ab53-11ef089d382e	JAMES EARL JONES	Raised from age 5 on his grandparents' farm in Dublin, Michigan (near Manistee); developed a severe stutter that kept him nearly silent through grade school until a high-school English teacher had him recite poetry aloud to break it. Graduated University of Michigan with a drama degree.
60858c47-487e-4b76-b5b8-2c7223f3aef3	a-ha	a-ha's video for "Take On Me" (1985) used rotoscope animation — roughly 3,000 frames hand-traced over live footage — taking 16 weeks and winning six MTV VMAs in 1986.
61804cff-b815-47a3-828a-6dcc937cd6a9	LAKE BAIKAL	World's oldest lake (25-30M years) and deepest (5,387 ft), holding ~20% of Earth's unfrozen fresh water — more than all 5 Great Lakes combined. Only place with a seal species living exclusively in fresh water — the Baikal seal (nerpa).
61c8e4f8-88de-4ebe-9030-9d23c88491c7	My Sharona	Doug Fieger — the Detroit-born, Oak Park-raised frontman of The Knack who wrote and sang 1979's biggest US hit "My Sharona" — was the kid brother of Geoffrey Fieger, the flamboyant Southfield lawyer who defended Jack Kevorkian. One Michigan family: the #1 song of 1979 and Dr. Death's attorney.
61e5b836-8c81-4d4a-944f-f2212943b8aa	Grease	John Travolta was 23 playing 18-year-old Danny Zuko in the 1978 film; Olivia Newton-John, playing 17-year-old Sandy, actually turned 29 during production.
62157959-89b5-4d13-bc0b-5fbffaab3954	Donald Keck	The modern internet runs partly on glass fiber invented by a Lansing, Michigan native. Donald Keck, who earned all three of his degrees from Michigan State University, worked with Robert Maurer and Peter Schultz at Corning in 1970 to create the first optical fiber clear enough for long-distance telecommunications — the trio received the National Medal of Technology for it in 2000.
6299e890-2691-4c7f-83cf-df946986cedd	NERD	The earliest print appearance of "nerd" is Dr. Seuss's 1950 book *If I Ran the Zoo*, just the name of a fictional creature. By Oct. 1951, Newsweek was already using it as slang for a socially awkward person.
62c7cd6f-af12-4d5a-bee0-998abc948029	THE HINDENBURG	LZ 129 was named after German President Paul von Hindenburg, who as president appointed Hitler chancellor in 1933. Caught fire docking at Lakehurst, NJ on May 6, 1937, burned in ~32 seconds, killing 36 — ended the passenger airship era.
62f54659-0acb-4577-91d5-904524befd5f	AVOCADO	From Nahuatl (Aztec) *āhuacatl*, which literally also meant "testicle" — almost certainly a shape joke.
63df0d5f-68d0-45e3-9902-554809d99b0b	a crossword (puzzle)	In the month before D-Day, the Daily Telegraph's crossword answers included UTAH, OMAHA, OVERLORD, MULBERRY, and NEPTUNE — the invasion's top-secret code names. MI5 arrested the compiler, Leonard Dawe, a Surrey school headmaster, and interrogated him as a suspected spy before clearing it as coincidence. The deeper layer: in 1984 former pupil Ronald French revealed Dawe had schoolboys fill in his blank grids — boys who hung around nearby US and Canadian troop camps soaking up soldiers' loose talk.
63f0b3c1-0987-4d24-b64c-3f0129d46240	The Butterfly Effect	MIT meteorologist Edward Lorenz coined the term while studying why weather forecasts kept failing — he found that rounding a single decimal in his computer model produced wildly different long-term weather. He named it after the title of his 1972 academic talk asking whether the flap of a butterfly's wings in Brazil could set off a tornado in Texas (he'd originally used a seagull, but was talked into the butterfly for poetry's sake).
640aad08-ec35-4ea0-b9ca-8d90d58572a5	Graham cracker	Graham crackers were invented in 1829 by Presbyterian minister Sylvester Graham, not as a treat but as a bland "anti-lust" health food — he believed rich, flavorful diets fueled sexual desire and moral decay, and preached a whole-wheat, bland-food regimen (plus cold showers and hard mattresses) to curb it.
6440dc75-eceb-4d64-8de2-bb2859c8bd73	PEZ	PEZ dispensers were designed in 1948 to look like cigarette lighters — the candy was marketed to European adults as a smoking-cessation aid under the slogan 'Smoking prohibited, PEZing allowed.' The cartoon character heads everyone collects today weren't added until the brand relaunched for American kids in the 1950s.
64a62281-deb1-4b00-a8c1-27ede153d23d	GROOT	Vin Diesel personally recorded "I am Groot" himself in at least 15 different languages for Marvel's global marketing, plus hundreds of English takes with different inflections.
652309e3-25e7-4e07-bf46-8f31e1f377dc	The Harlem Globetrotters	Founded by Abe Saperstein in 1926 with players from Chicago's South Side — they had no connection to Harlem and didn't play a single game there until 1968, over 40 years in. Saperstein picked the name because Harlem was the capital of Black culture and an out-of-town name added mystique.
6552ff50-0d48-4257-8544-7268a1e62b0c	BIG WHEEL	The low-slung plastic tricycle with the giant front wheel was introduced by Louis Marx and Company in 1969, inducted into the National Toy Hall of Fame in 2009.
655bc0bc-b9c7-4b77-9a74-843b3acf5d03	Petoskey Stone	Michigan's official state stone is fossilized coral — Hexagonaria percarinata — from a warm shallow tropical sea that covered the whole state roughly 350 million years ago during the Devonian. Gov. George Romney signed it into law in 1965.
659579fe-1e92-42ad-a4e9-c45526c34d46	Rawhide	Ronald Reagan's Secret Service codename was "Rawhide" — chosen because Reagan kept horses at his California ranch.
671ee02e-effb-40c1-8c0d-d3dc9e27a02c	GRITTY	The Philadelphia Flyers' mascot debuted Sept. 24, 2018, divisive enough at first that The Guardian called him "an acid trip of a mascot" — within two months he had more Twitter followers than any other NHL mascot, then was voted #1 NHL mascot by the players' union in 2019.
67373a87-ea2e-4d5b-bdc7-41b743435a93	RAIN	"Precipitation" is a "kangaroo word" — it contains the letters of a shorter synonym, "rain," in the same left-to-right order, hiding inside it like a joey in its mother's pouch.
6762946c-796b-4534-a207-66f896ef66ef	BATMAN V SUPERMAN: DAWN OF JUSTICE	The Batman/Superman fight scenes were filmed inside Michigan Central Station — Detroit's abandoned 1913 train depot, empty and vandalized for nearly three decades. Production spent ~$131M in Michigan.
67bea910-8bae-479d-b271-538598aa408c	JAWS	The 25-foot mechanical shark built for *Jaws* (1975) was nicknamed "Bruce" by the crew, after Bruce Ramer, Spielberg's real-life lawyer. Three functioning Bruces were built; one survives, restored, hanging in the Academy Museum atrium in LA.
691ffcac-c635-4144-aa89-4af4ac16cd46	Little Caesars	Little Caesars started in 1959 in a Garden City, Michigan strip mall, funded by Mike and Marian Ilitch's $10,000 in life savings. Mike — a former Detroit Tigers minor-league shortstop — wanted to call it 'Pizza Treat'; Marian talked him into 'Little Caesars,' her own nickname for her husband.
6946406b-add8-4d64-8c86-daedd4136371	Cashew	Raw cashew shells contain urushiol — the same skin-blistering toxin found in poison ivy — which is why cashews are never sold in the shell like most other nuts; every one you've ever eaten was steamed or roasted first to neutralize it.
69b9fc53-c347-480d-a9b5-1ff167d573e5	LARRY PAGE	Google co-founder born in East Lansing; father Carl Page was an MSU computer science professor. Earned his undergrad engineering degree at Michigan before Stanford.
6a439bd1-559b-4c9f-b71c-691de4eb9dbc	Jethro Tull	At the first-ever hard rock/metal Grammy (Feb 22, 1989), the flute-fronted prog band beat favorite Metallica — the room laughed thinking it was a joke, Entertainment Weekly later called it the biggest upset in Grammy history, and the backlash forced NARAS to split hard rock and metal into separate categories in 1990. Tull's label Chrysalis ran a Billboard ad: "The flute is a heavy, metal instrument."
6b029bf0-52e7-4f64-9759-f4b1b3c45781	TRANS-SIBERIAN RAILWAY	At ~9,289 km, the Moscow-to-Vladivostok line is the longest railway in the world, historically crossing a record eight time zones.
6d8dd55b-477c-45aa-8a63-3d0fcf0c92a6	LINCOLN LOGS	Invented ~1916-1918 by John Lloyd Wright, son of architect Frank Lloyd Wright, patented 1920 ("Toy-Cabin Construction," US Patent 1,351,086).
6df67f7c-71a1-488c-8a4c-d3b936983257	Mark Moseley	In the strike-shortened 1982 season, Washington kicker Mark Moseley made 20 of 21 field goals and was named NFL MVP — the only placekicker ever to win the award. He's one of just a handful of non-quarterbacks to take it home in the award's 65-plus-year history.
6e8aef59-4e26-430d-93d0-efd3c9756ab3	Dirty Dancing	Made for about $6 million after being rejected by studios dozens of times, "Dirty Dancing" became the first film ever to sell over a million home-video copies — 1.2 million in the first 90 days after its March 1988 VHS release.
6e9b54cf-1c6e-4469-9d21-fa666c404bbc	The Michigan Triangle	A stretch of Lake Michigan between Ludington, Manitowoc, and Benton Harbor has picked up its own "Bermuda Triangle"-style reputation after Northwest Orient Airlines Flight 2501 vanished there on June 23, 1950, killing all 58 aboard in what was then the deadliest commercial airline crash in US history. Debris and body fragments washed up — but the plane itself has never been found, despite searches continuing into the 2020s.
6ef4b323-12b5-4557-898a-790a839afc03	GRETA GARBO	MI5's top WW2 double agent, Spaniard Juan Pujol García, was so convincing feeding the Nazis fake intelligence (500+ fabricated reports, helping convince Hitler D-Day was aimed at Calais not Normandy) his British handlers codenamed him after Garbo — "the best actor in the world." Decorated by both sides: an Iron Cross from Germany and an MBE from Britain, both 1944.
71e0c544-42d8-44c4-9706-0a669d07a88c	Chortle	"Chortle" isn't an old word — Lewis Carroll invented it in 1871 for "Through the Looking-Glass," blending "chuckle" and "snort."
725af991-8196-4525-8f7b-ef70dd458c38	Gibraltar	Gibraltar — the British Rock at the mouth of the Mediterranean — shares a land border with Spain that's just 0.75 miles (1.2 km) long, the entire boundary of the territory.
72a6156b-2bce-4e97-9cbe-71650db762d1	Bing Crosby	The only complete recording of Game 7 of the 1960 World Series — Bill Mazeroski's walk-off — exists because Bing Crosby, part-owner of the Pirates, was too superstitious to watch live. He listened by shortwave radio in Paris while a kinescope was recorded by Ampex (a company he'd invested in), watched it once, and left it in his wine cellar, where it sat untouched for 49 years until a marketing VP found the five-reel set in December 2009. Networks of the era taped over sports broadcasts; games 1–6 are lost.
72ac4962-2e27-431c-a47e-c7dd8d42e75e	Alice Cooper	In 1973, Salvador Dali — a fan who'd caught Alice Cooper live — invited him to sit for a hologram titled "First Cylindric Chromo-Hologram Portrait of Alice Cooper's Brain." Cooper posed in a million dollars' worth of Harry Winston diamonds, cradling a Venus de Milo statuette, while behind him a plaster brain topped with a chocolate eclair crawled with live ants. The piece still rotates on display at the Dali museums in Figueres, Spain, and St. Petersburg, Florida.
72ea3dbd-435a-4b15-bd90-bafc3455b769	HOT WHEELS	Mattel's Elliot Handler created Hot Wheels in 1968 after his son wanted souped-up hot-rod fantasy cars instead of realistic Matchbox cars. A specially lubricated thin axle let them outrun Matchbox.
72fab1e0-c3a3-4425-aa01-f4766bfd9386	Bikini Atoll	This Marshall Islands coral reef was the site of dozens of US nuclear weapons tests starting in 1946 — the same week French designer Louis Réard unveiled his daring new two-piece swimsuit in Paris. He deliberately named it "the bikini," betting the reaction to it would be as explosive as the bomb tests making headlines.
7388028f-454a-4ef9-9083-61910f7f67a7	a coconut	After a Japanese destroyer rammed and sank PT-109 in August 1943, Lt. John F. Kennedy carved a rescue message into a coconut husk — "NAURO ISL... 11 ALIVE... NEED SMALL BOAT... KENNEDY" — and handed it to two Solomon Islanders to paddle to the nearest PT base. His father later had the husk encased in wood and plastic, and it sat on JFK's Oval Office desk as a paperweight for his entire presidency — it was on the desk the day he died. It's now at the JFK Library.
73acdd3d-4839-493c-8077-621e1756eec4	Jamal Crawford	Jamal Crawford, a three-time NBA Sixth Man of the Year, played 1,327 career games without ever fouling out — the longest streak in league history. Wilt Chamberlain is the closest competitor, going his entire 1,045-game career without six fouls, but Crawford played 282 more games than that without it ever happening.
73b3cf5b-639c-4b5d-a82a-6875752a623d	Amway	Amway was founded in 1959 in Ada, Michigan, by Jay Van Andel and Rich DeVos, selling a single all-purpose cleaner out of their homes. DeVos went on to buy the NBA's Orlando Magic in 1991, and the DeVos family still owns the team.
748527aa-717b-44a9-84f3-115b92a1b619	Maus	Art Spiegelman's Maus is the only graphic novel ever to win a Pulitzer Prize — a Special Award in 1992, created specifically because the book didn't fit any existing category. It tells his father's true story of surviving Auschwitz and Dachau, drawn with Jews as mice and Nazis as cats.
74e1536a-c2a1-491c-abc9-47f4bd6fa597	American Pie (the movie)	American Pie (1999) is set in Michigan — the fictional "East Great Falls" is a thin mask for East Grand Rapids: writer Adam Herz based the script on his own years at East Grand Rapids High School, and the film's working title was literally "East Grand Rapids." The script sold for a reported $650,000.
75adb024-e21a-4b85-81ad-afc82e3c91ac	Dord	"Dord" is a fake word that lived in Webster's New International Dictionary (2nd edition) for five years. In 1931, an editor submitted a card reading "D or d, cont./density" meaning the letters D or d can abbreviate "density" — a typesetter misread the spaced-out "D or d" as one solid word, "dord," and it got defined and printed as a synonym for density. Nobody caught it until 1939, and the correction wasn't published until 1947.
75eafd53-1f95-4e5e-97ca-ae72c4223f96	March Madness	The NCAA didn't coin "March Madness" — Illinois high school sports official Henry V. Porter did, in a 1939 essay describing the state's high school basketball tournament. The NCAA only got tangled up in the phrase after a 1996 trademark lawsuit from the Illinois High School Association, ending with a joint trademark entity; the NCAA didn't gain full ownership until 2012.
7600a49d-0e47-45a7-8615-f5a822e68dea	Talking Heads	Talking Heads got their name from a TV Guide glossary of broadcast jargon — "talking head" was industry term for a static head-and-shoulders shot of someone speaking on camera.
763e8507-7d66-491a-83a6-7e0cd13f6586	The Exorcist	Director William Friedkin had a $50,000 refrigeration system installed to cool the bedroom set to -20°F so actors' breath would show onscreen — it was so cold they could only shoot 3 minutes at a time before it had to re-chill.
77541e3a-a89d-4455-a9d7-7e8ee651798b	M\*A\*S\*H	The Korean War sitcom never filmed in Korea — every exterior, including the famous helicopter-flyover opening, was shot at the old 20th Century Fox Ranch (now Malibu Creek State Park), California, 1972–1983.
77565d67-6e52-4b15-a953-61e182949d6b	Westworld	Before Jurassic Park, novelist/director Michael Crichton pioneered a different kind of on-screen monster tech: his 1973 film Westworld is credited as the first feature film to use computer-generated imagery — a blocky, pixelated ~2-minute sequence showing the point of view of the malfunctioning "Gunslinger" android, visually inspired by real NASA Mariner Mars flyby photos.
778907d9-596b-40b6-9e86-2ae77233644c	Norm (Norm Peterson, Cheers)	Norm's real first name is Hilary. Revealed in Cheers S9E21 "It's a Wonderful Wife" (March 1991) — Vera, working as the Melville's hatcheck girl, blurts "Norm's real first name is Hilary!" Norm claims grandfather Hilary "once killed a man who laughed at him"… then admits: "Not exactly. He was a surgeon. He sort of botched an operation."
77bb15af-f99a-429a-9e30-130c97bf86b3	Chucky	Child's Play creator Don Mancini assembled Chucky's human name — Charles Lee Ray — from three real killers: Charles Manson, Lee Harvey Oswald, and James Earl Ray. The doll's "Good Guy" persona has carried the stitched-together name since the 1988 original.
78f065da-e080-4c97-b6d6-e315830f0e7e	Stevie Wonder	His 1963 chart-topper "Fingertips (Pt. 2)" — cut live at Chicago's Regal Theater during a Motortown Revue — was the first live recording ever to hit #1 on the Hot 100 (and the first single to top the pop and R&B charts simultaneously). The record preserves pure chaos: Wonder faked an ending, the next act's bassist Joe Swift had already taken the stage, and you can hear him yell "What key? What key?" when the encore starts. The drummer on the track: a pre-fame Marvin Gaye.
7a3b2f8e-b8dd-4dd9-820c-eac5914a5904	HARRY HOUDINI	Died in Detroit on Halloween — Oct. 31, 1926 — at Grace Hospital, of peritonitis from a ruptured appendix, days after a college student sucker-punched him in the stomach in Montreal.
7a9c315e-0d5a-484e-a12d-90aa3a18a225	Kellogg's Corn Flakes	MYTH (rated "mostly false" by Snopes): the popular claim that Kellogg's Corn Flakes were invented specifically to stop masturbation. John Harvey Kellogg did preach bland diets to curb sexual desire, and Corn Flakes grew out of his Battle Creek Sanitarium's health philosophy — but he never marketed Corn Flakes for that purpose, and the exact "anti-masturbatory breakfast" phrasing traces to a 2012 Mental Floss article, not Kellogg himself.
7ad305fc-cf84-4c2d-8816-8dbd7a006cf4	Alien	The original 1979 Alien costume head contains a REAL human skull — H.R. Giger bought human skulls (sourced from India for medical use), riveted one onto the front of the creature's head as its "face," sawed off the jawbone and extended it about six inches, then hid it under the smooth translucent dome. Co-writer Dan O'Bannon confirmed it in Fantastic Films: "The face of the thing is a real human skull."
7b3ad022-213a-439c-8ef6-eb6feb5b1ec7	The Stanley Cup	There is no annual replica of the Stanley Cup — teams pass the same trophy along — and it has led a rough life. In 1924 the champion Montreal Canadiens got a flat tire, pulled the Cup out of the trunk to reach the spare, drove off, and left hockey's holiest object sitting in a roadside snowbank until they doubled back for it.
7b615487-2ea7-48b7-8d8d-a5a80432af8f	Operation Mincemeat	In 1943, British intelligence dressed the corpse of a homeless Welshman as fictional "Major William Martin," planted fake invasion documents on him, and released him from a submarine off Spain — tricking Germany into shifting reinforcements away from the real target, Sicily.
7bade62d-2cd8-4623-9e82-b242f060cc1d	Disaster	"Disaster" comes from Italian "disastro" — literally "ill-starred," from dis- (bad) + astro (star) — reflecting the old astrological belief that calamity came from an unfavorable star. Entered English in the 1590s.
7bb27c2f-9a9a-41bd-aaf8-172150985bd6	ROSA PARKS	After the Montgomery bus boycott, moved to Detroit in 1957 and lived there the rest of her life, working decades as an aide in Congressman John Conyers's Detroit office (1965-1988).
7bbf4d26-84e7-44a5-a1ef-46d483406e8d	Sterling Holloway	The man who voiced Winnie the Pooh for nearly 40 years also voiced the Cheshire Cat in Alice in Wonderland, Kaa in The Jungle Book, and Roquefort in The Aristocats.
7c96c021-70c5-4c43-beb5-9b82883f0318	Tennis for Two	Widely considered the first video game ever built purely for entertainment, 'Tennis for Two' was created in 1958 by physicist William Higinbotham at Brookhaven National Laboratory — a nuclear research lab — using an analog computer and a 5-inch oscilloscope screen. He built it in a few hours to give visitors something fun to do on the lab's annual public day.
7dbf9721-5b41-4049-bacf-5e7247e0b676	Michelin	The Michelin Guide was invented by the tire company as a free motoring handbook in 1900 — restaurant stars didn't appear until 1926. The whole scheme existed to make people drive more and wear out tires faster. It went from free to paid in 1920 after André Michelin found copies propping up a workbench in a tire shop.
7efa5a4b-7e84-47c8-a644-68180f128be8	Danny Biasone (the 24-second NBA shot clock)	The NBA's 24-second shot clock came from a bowling-alley owner's back-of-the-napkin math: Danny Biasone took the 48 minutes in a game, converted to 2,880 seconds, divided by the 120 shots he figured both teams should combine to take, and got 24. The league adopted it in 1954 and scoring jumped almost overnight.
7f805238-aeb1-48ae-9f8b-0af8a11cfc67	SATURDAY NIGHT LIVE	Didn't launch under its own name — debuted Oct. 11, 1975 as "NBC's Saturday Night," because ABC already owned the "Saturday Night Live" trademark for a rival Howard Cosell variety show.
7fe5ee69-e745-40fe-bd7e-717c53e394c1	MARY POPPINS	Author P.L. Travers was so furious about Disney's film — especially mixing live actors with cartoon penguins — that at the 1964 premiere she confronted Walt Disney demanding the animated sequence be cut. He brushed her off ("Pamela, the ship has sailed") and walked away.
8042cf4f-9f43-46a5-8a8f-a1d6adec52ef	Elvis Presley	On December 21, 1970, Elvis Presley showed up unannounced at the White House with a six-page handwritten letter asking President Nixon to make him a federal "Agent-at-Large" in the fight against drugs. Nixon met with him and granted him an honorary narcotics badge. The photo from that meeting is now the single most requested image in the history of the U.S. National Archives — outranking requests for the Constitution and the Bill of Rights.
80819c31-5445-49c0-b44b-082d7e93ed4b	Low Rider	The song's stuttering rhythm exists because drummer Harold Brown flubbed a take, playing the upbeat instead of the downbeat - rather than redo it, the band kept going, and sax player Charles Miller ad-libbed the "Low rider" line over it.
812ab98f-6bf0-4485-9c73-f8eb1973668a	Tommy Tutone ("867-5309/Jenny")	When Tommy Tutone released "867-5309/Jenny" in 1981, real households and businesses holding that exact number in dozens of area codes got flooded with calls for decades. An Alabama woman had to change her number in 1982 after 2 a.m. calls; a New Jersey DJ who later owned the digits estimated he got roughly 10,000 calls a year asking for Jenny. Two rival plumbing companies eventually fought publicly over commercial rights to advertise using 867-5309.
813d2a68-62e3-4724-92b3-b52ce1140119	Chupacabra	A five-year investigation by Benjamin Radford (book: "Tracking the Chupacabra," 2011) traced the entire legend to one 1995 Puerto Rico eyewitness, Madelyne Tolentino — whose creature description (spikes down the back, alien eyes, two-legged gait) matches Sil, the monster from the sci-fi film "Species," which she admitted seeing in the weeks before her sighting. The world's youngest famous cryptid is, in effect, a movie review.
81d441af-d3e2-47ff-b135-70aed4b1a8f1	SONIC THE HEDGEHOG	Sega's Naoto Ohshima fused Felix the Cat's head onto Mickey Mouse's body, field-testing sketches on strangers in Central Park. Shoes pulled from Michael Jackson's *Bad*-album boots and Santa's color scheme.
81d5d0bc-f0b2-4b17-b6df-47a1762c51c4	Weezer	Rivers Cuomo's dad nicknamed him "Weezer" as a kid — after Wheezer, a character from the 1920s-30s "Our Gang"/Little Rascals comedy shorts his father thought was "the cool one." Cuomo grabbed the childhood nickname (dropping the H) under time pressure, naming the band right before their first-ever gig: opening for Dogstar — Keanu Reeves's real rock band.
81e0ce06-9263-4989-8c64-953d3ee7df90	TABASCO	Tabasco mash ages up to three years in white oak barrels on Avery Island, Louisiana — barrels previously used to age whiskey, decharred and cleaned before reuse.
81e8932e-e9a5-46b7-b8a2-d13fc29cbcbf	HENRY FORD	January 1914, Ford announced the "Five Dollar Day" — more than doubling the standard wage for Detroit-area workers, a year after rolling out the moving assembly line.
81fe1deb-53a3-44f8-a883-3b4b578ef515	Panic! at the Disco	Nearly everyone assumes the name (and exclamation point) comes from The Smiths' 1986 song "Panic," whose chorus commands "burn down the disco" — a perfect match. But per the band's own account, the real source was an obscure late-90s Orange County band called Name Taken, whose song "Panic" the teenage founders were actually into.
82cc33cd-ba1f-4531-9a47-fc58ffe41a9f	ROUTE 66	Ran 2,448 miles Chicago to Santa Monica; nicknamed "the Mother Road" by John Steinbeck in *The Grapes of Wrath* (1939).
8322c4e7-6f18-4876-9b8c-ef7bbabf746c	Uno	Uno was invented in 1971 by Reading, Ohio barber Merle Robbins to settle a family argument over the rules of Crazy Eights. He and his family mortgaged their house to print the first 5,000 decks, sold them out of his barbershop, then sold the rights a year later for just $50,000 plus a dime-a-deck royalty.
83327653-f3c6-4627-9be7-fd64f187994e	MOUNT EVEREST	Named after Sir George Everest, British Surveyor General of India, who reportedly never saw it and objected to the honor, telling the Royal Geographical Society in 1857 that "Everest" couldn't be written in Hindi or pronounced by Indians.
833fd914-d2f3-4153-a7bc-1adb3a899245	The Rose Bowl	The first one (January 1, 1902) was Michigan 49, Stanford 0 — Stanford's captain asked to quit with 8 minutes still on the clock. The blowout was so bad the Tournament of Roses dropped football entirely for 13 years, running Ben-Hur-style chariot races and ostrich races instead, until football returned for good in 1916.
83c73195-78a1-4f35-92a0-d29a4a6b291e	RASPUTIN	The famous legend of Rasputin's 1916 death (cyanide cakes, multiple gunshots, beaten, drowned alive) comes almost entirely from killer Prince Yusupov's own memoirs — the real autopsy found no poison, no water in his lungs; a single close-range gunshot killed him, Dec. 30, 1916.
8406532d-3b44-4d0f-98ed-6d90efc90c04	BERMUDA TRIANGLE	The USS Cyclops vanished there in 1918 with 306 crew — still the largest single loss of life in US Navy history outside combat — and in 1945 five Navy TBM Avenger bombers (Flight 19) disappeared on a routine training flight from Fort Lauderdale.
84f70cf4-ee93-4f71-ac2b-07f937929b10	Steppenwolf	The "Born to Be Wild" band is named after Hermann Hesse's 1927 novel Steppenwolf, on their producer Gabriel Mekler's suggestion after he'd read the book.
85394827-0f10-497a-a6d2-1571b30f7262	Pokémon	Episode 38 of Pokémon aired exactly once — December 16, 1997 — and sent 685 Japanese viewers to hospitals by ambulance with photosensitive seizures ("Pokémon Shock"). The show went on a four-month hiatus, Japan wrote new broadcast standards, and the episode has never aired again anywhere. The kicker: the seizure-triggering explosion was caused by Pikachu's Thunderbolt, but the episode was titled for Porygon — who took the fall and effectively never appeared in the anime again.
87b42e78-1008-4365-9af1-2a99000d05fa	Maleficent	The disembodied head chanting inside the Haunted Mansion's crystal ball speaks with the voice of Maleficent — Disney brought Eleanor Audley, who voiced the Sleeping Beauty villain (1959), out to Disneyland to record Madame Leota's séance incantations for the 1969 attraction.
87c112e8-85d8-4821-b2b9-208ed2026ed0	Sriracha / Huy Fong Foods (David Tran)	The rooster on the Sriracha bottle isn't a random mascot — it's founder David Tran's Chinese zodiac sign. Tran, a Vietnamese refugee, named his company Huy Fong Foods after the cargo freighter that carried him out of Vietnam in 1979; he started selling the hot sauce out of Los Angeles's Chinatown the following year.
87f3bca7-3751-426b-9a10-6149f36ba112	Silly Putty	Silly Putty was a WWII rubber shortage that failed upward. In 1943, General Electric engineer James Wright was trying to synthesize a substitute for military rubber (Japan controlled the world's natural rubber supply) when he mixed boric acid into silicone oil and got a bouncy, stretchy dud that was useless as rubber. It sat around as a novelty until a 1950 New Yorker mention triggered 250,000 orders in three days — and in 1968, Apollo 8 astronauts carried a wad of it to lunar orbit in a silver egg, using it to keep tools from floating away in zero gravity.
882246d1-b08d-4347-b38b-4b04a3cceff1	DIEGO RIVERA (Detroit Industry Murals)	Rivera spent 1932-33 painting 27 fresco panels at the Detroit Institute of Arts depicting Ford's River Rouge plant, commissioned with $20,000 from Edsel Ford, who later personally defended the murals when denounced as un-American. Rivera called it his most successful work.
884e103c-43b2-4e8f-85ab-8e7567297590	NERF	The Nerf ball launched in 1970 as "the world's first official indoor ball" — the name comes from "nerf bars," protective off-road-racing tubing.
88924456-3c51-4139-bb8a-9fe209989b01	HOW THE GRINCH STOLE CHRISTMAS! (1966)	"You're a Mean One, Mr. Grinch" was NOT sung by Boris Karloff (narrator/Grinch voice) — it was bass singer Thurl Ravenscroft, whose name was accidentally left off the original credits.
88cf8d68-bf99-43a0-9b2c-e3568fd8eeac	WD-40	The name is literally the lab notebook entry: "Water Displacement, 40th attempt" — chemist Norm Larsen's Rocket Chemical Company got the anti-rust formula right on try #40 in 1953, built to keep rust off Atlas missile skins for aerospace contractor Convair.
8922d88c-eb8c-40d5-8821-dbce545d61ad	Mr. Brightside (The Killers)	The song has no second verse — Brandon Flowers just sings the first verse twice. He admitted "I didn't have any other lines, and it ended up sticking" (Rolling Stone, 2018); the accidental loop now reads as the point, a jealous brain replaying the same scene. That song went on to become the longest-charting single in UK chart history — 508 weeks in the Top 100 as of June 2026 — without ever reaching No. 1.
896786f6-6570-40e7-ad95-26e6cf231fb4	Agatha Christie	Guinness World Records names Agatha Christie the best-selling fiction author of all time, with an estimated 2 billion copies of her 78 crime novels sold across 44 languages — outsold historically only by the Bible and Shakespeare.
898c3b75-a2a1-454f-88bb-18f3f2f8fd39	Twinkie	The Twinkie exists because of idle machinery. In 1930, Chicago-area bakery manager James Dewar noticed the equipment used to inject cream into strawberry shortcakes sat unused outside berry season, so he adapted it to pipe banana cream into a small sponge cake instead. Wartime banana rationing later forced a permanent switch to vanilla.
89f4772f-8d04-4649-b591-36a7527c85af	Deadline	Before meaning a newsroom's final-copy time (by 1919), "dead line" was a literal Civil War prison-camp term — a boundary drawn 15–20 feet inside a stockade wall that guards shot any prisoner for crossing.
8ae5f3d0-2d33-412a-975f-b04dc0c12de9	MARATHON	The 26.2-mile marathon distance wasn't set by the ancient Greeks — fixed at the 1908 London Olympics when organizers extended the planned 26-mile route to start on the Windsor Castle lawn (Queen Alexandra's request, so the royal children could watch) and finish before the royal box, adding 385 extra yards.
8b2474b7-e4df-43e1-a4d1-886e198161ad	Mountain Dew	Mountain Dew's name is a wink at moonshine — 'mountain dew' was 19th-century Appalachian slang for illegal corn whiskey. Tennessee bottlers the Hartman brothers created the lemon-lime soda in the 1940s specifically as a whiskey mixer, and early labels featured a barefoot hillbilly toting a rifle and a jug.
8b7da908-a2b8-438b-b922-2f2a6896f8ad	Apollo 13 (the 1995 film)	Ron Howard shot the weightless scenes for real — NASA let the production film aboard its KC-135 "Vomit Comet" reduced-gravity trainer, flying 612 parabolic arcs over 13 days of flights to bank just under four hours of true zero-g footage, captured in bursts of roughly 20-23 seconds per dive. A cramped Apollo capsule set was built inside the aircraft's fuselage.
8b92dfcf-0b12-4f5b-ad26-59f7703393f1	"TAINTED LOVE" (Soft Cell)	Not written for Soft Cell — a 1964 Northern Soul B-side by American singer Gloria Jones. Soft Cell's 1981 cover set a then-record 43 consecutive weeks on the Billboard Hot 100 and was the UK's best-selling single of 1981.
8c0b9a42-fbc1-47f0-9171-774d2fd1d96f	The Empire Strikes Back	A graphic artist named Lee Seiler sued Lucasfilm claiming George Lucas stole his "Garthian Striders" designs for the AT-AT Imperial Walkers. He lost badly - he couldn't produce any originals predating the film's 1980 release, and the Ninth Circuit found he'd testified falsely and destroyed evidence trying to fake a paper trail.
8cbde71f-21fe-47d5-ade0-a7d782c45af5	Bell's Two Hearted Ale	Bell's Brewery's flagship IPA is named for Michigan's real Two Hearted River in the Upper Peninsula — the same river Ernest Hemingway made famous in his 1925 short story "Big Two-Hearted River." Except Hemingway wasn't actually writing about it: literary historians agree he was describing the Fox River near Seney, but borrowed the more evocative "Two Hearted" name for his title anyway.
8dab155f-b70b-468d-8d16-f8896c09d6eb	The Brazil Nut Effect	Shake a can of mixed nuts and the big Brazil nuts always end up on top — real, studied physics called granular convection, where vibration makes larger particles rise through smaller ones even though gravity should do the opposite.
8ef44d6c-bc0c-442f-8df1-597f2489cdcc	Buick	Before building cars, Scotsman David Dunbar Buick made his fortune in Detroit inventing a process for permanently bonding porcelain enamel to cast iron — the method still used to make white bathtubs today. He sold the plumbing business and sank the money into gasoline engines, incorporating Buick Motor Company in 1903.
8fcf380e-d0f4-4c46-8689-0715981aa612	Nolan Ryan	On July 15, 1973 at Tiger Stadium, with two outs in the bottom of the ninth of Nolan Ryan's second no-hitter (17 strikeouts), Tigers first baseman Norm Cash walked to the plate carrying a table leg from the clubhouse instead of a bat. Umpire Ron Luciano made him swap it; Cash protested "I've got as much chance with this as I do with a bat," made the final out, and told Luciano "See, I told ya."
901696c9-d105-42c4-a507-b3984e3a7fd2	PHILLIE PHANATIC	Debuted April 25, 1978 at Veterans Stadium, designed by Bonnie Erickson and husband Wayde Harrison.
90e1af53-3a9e-4888-bce9-7009267adf9b	Jelly Belly	Jelly Belly didn't have a blue jelly bean until 1980 — invented specifically to complete a red-white-and-blue lineup for Ronald Reagan's 1981 inauguration. Roughly 3.5 tons of Jelly Bellys shipped to DC for the festivities, and a jar sat on Reagan's desk throughout his presidency.
90ee29ab-d35c-4992-a649-de7528d0ded7	Alice Cooper	Shock-rock pioneer Alice Cooper (born Vincent Furnier) was born in Detroit, Michigan in 1948.
9197f03a-550b-496e-8f2c-ff84ff1ac2c8	CLUE	The 1985 movie *Clue* released with three different endings — each theater randomly got a print with only ONE, so audiences believed they'd seen the real conclusion. Home video was the first place all three played back to back.
91c1a8a7-252a-4e3e-b3f2-e7863b20f385	Garth Brooks	In 1999, at the peak of his country stardom, Garth Brooks invented a fictional alternative-rock persona named Chris Gaines — mullet, soul patch, and an invented backstory as an Australian rock star — to headline a movie ("The Lamb") that was never actually made. He released a full "greatest hits" album under the Gaines identity as a pre-release soundtrack for the nonexistent film.
91ccd347-e3ff-457d-a122-21b94c9db6dc	France and Spain	A tiny, uninhabited river island called Pheasant Island — the world's smallest condominium — is jointly governed by France and Spain, switching sovereignty every six months: Spain administers it Feb-Jul, France Aug-Jan, an arrangement running continuously since the 1659 Treaty of the Pyrenees.
91ed790b-d67c-498e-b78f-7e9b46bd0e1d	eBay	eBay's famous founding myth says Pierre Omidyar built the site in 1995 to help his fiancee trade Pez dispensers. Fabricated by an early eBay spokesperson because "nobody wants to hear about a 30-year-old genius who wanted to create a perfect market" - the real first item ever sold was a broken laser pointer, for $14.83.
9273597d-54f0-4245-b404-47e330b53452	THE SHAWSHANK REDEMPTION	Cost ~$25M, made only ~$29M worldwide on initial release — a flop, largely blamed on its forgettable title. Now IMDb's #1 rated film of all time (9.3/10, 3M+ voters).
92832223-a090-4fe6-aa39-e0b3cd8acf2e	Sixpence None the Richer	The one-hit wonder behind 1999's "Kiss Me" named itself after a line in C.S. Lewis's "Mere Christianity," his illustration of a child buying his father a gift with the father's own money, leaving the father no richer, but pleased all the same.
92d721e7-f3ba-4c24-ae68-f2262956c2c9	Popsicle	The Popsicle was invented by accident by an 11-year-old. In 1905 Frank Epperson left a cup of powdered-soda water — with the stirring stick still in it — outside on a cold San Francisco Bay Area night. It froze solid. He did not patent his 'Epsicle' until 1923.
9315fb32-e55b-487a-a883-adf63ac948f7	Cotton candy	Cotton candy was co-invented by a dentist. Nashville dentist William Morrison and confectioner John Wharton built the first electric spun-sugar machine in 1897 and sold it as 'Fairy Floss' at the 1904 World's Fair, moving over 68,000 boxes. The name 'cotton candy' came later — from yet another dentist.
94491d02-00ec-4a9f-8bf5-5041bd328f80	WYATT EARP	The 1881 gunfight at the O.K. Corral that made him a legend lasted about 30 seconds.
94d57aa7-4da2-40e2-8d4a-6820f6608fb3	Alan Turing	British mathematician Alan Turing led the Bletchley Park team that built the Bombe machine to crack Germany's Enigma cipher in WW2. Despite this, he was prosecuted for "gross indecency" in 1952 for a same-sex relationship, sentenced to chemical castration, and died in 1954 (ruled suicide). He received a posthumous royal pardon from Queen Elizabeth II in 2013.
94d8f266-d1f1-4b5d-a1bb-97404b61f936	Nepal	Nepal's national flag is the only non-quadrilateral flag of any country on Earth — two stacked triangular pennants, with precise mathematical proportions written into the Nepali constitution since 1962.
94e29d6b-6c52-40fd-a35e-b1561c3869b6	Tickle Me Elmo	The 1996 holiday shortage of Tickle Me Elmo triggered real store violence — a Walmart employee in Canada suffered a broken rib, concussion, and pulled hamstring after being trampled by shoppers rushing the last units.
94efb684-960a-4073-93ed-0b3b08801fb9	RUBBER DUCK	January 10, 1992, a North Pacific storm washed 28,800 plastic bath toys off a cargo ship. Oceanographers tracked their scattering for over a decade to map global ocean currents; some hit Alaska within a year, bleached survivors still washing up in the UK by 2007.
94f081ae-9509-413b-b3dd-3a1712a6aaba	8 MILE (Eminem)	Named for a real Detroit road (8 Mile Road/M-102) that for decades was the informal racial dividing line between majority-Black Detroit and the largely white suburbs to the north — the exact fault line the semi-autobiographical film uses to frame Eminem's outsider status.
95077a41-3104-4a38-92c3-17a57f967973	Mrs. Doubtfire	Robin Williams's Mrs. Doubtfire makeup took roughly four hours to apply every single day — the latex pieces (nose, lips, eye bags, forehead) had to be recast from scratch each morning — and it won the Oscar for Best Makeup at the 1994 Academy Awards.
95c7812e-3733-46be-8281-648c29c7c734	MY CHEMICAL ROMANCE	Bassist Mikey Way named the band while working at a Barnes & Noble — spotted Irvine Welsh's novel *Ecstasy: Three Tales of Chemical Romance* on the shelf.
9666950f-5bb6-485e-a7ff-4866fd467002	PANCHO VILLA	His March 1916 raid on Columbus, New Mexico was one of the rare times a foreign military force directly attacked the continental US mainland.
970608ee-f54f-4c2c-b3c6-55fd8c9d4f1e	Devo	Devo's "de-evolution" concept traces directly to the May 4, 1970 Kent State shootings. Co-founder Gerry Casale was a Kent State student who witnessed the shooting and was friends with two of the four students killed, turning his half-joking college theory into the band's serious mission.
97192f17-51d2-4d11-a523-43bde70ceabf	MARIANO RIVERA	"Enter Sandman" wasn't his idea — a Yankee Stadium scoreboard staffer picked it in 1999 after seeing how hard Padres fans reacted to Trevor Hoffman's AC/DC entrance in the '98 World Series. Rivera also was the last active MLB player allowed to wear No. 42, grandfathered when MLB retired it league-wide in 1997, until his 2013 retirement.
98370ead-7280-4605-8bc4-02f56e6d07c7	PAC-MAN	Designer Toru Iwatani based the shape on the Japanese kanji for "mouth"; original Japanese title was "Puck Man," renamed for the US to stop vandals scratching the "P" into an "F" on cabinets.
983e4e2a-926c-4915-a3df-3f2a2a53b2b0	TUTANKHAMUN (KING TUT)	Howard Carter found the nearly intact tomb in 1922, bankrolled by the 5th Earl of Carnarvon.
985089ba-465c-4b11-922a-4ec45e696199	PURDUE BOILERMAKERS	Nickname isn't from the beer-and-shot cocktail — dates to an 1891 newspaper headline mocking Purdue's football team as "burly boiler makers" after a 44-0 win, tied to the school's industrial/railroad-shop training.
9918f172-4aca-476c-bfda-69a00c53eea4	The Rolling Stones	Mick Jagger commissioned the Rolling Stones' tongue-and-lips logo in 1970 after showing designer John Pasche a magazine image of the Hindu goddess Kali, whose defiant, tongue-out depiction inspired the design. Pasche was paid just 50 pounds up front (plus 200 pounds later) for what became one of the most recognizable logos in music history. He eventually sold his copyright to the band's commercial arm for 26,000 pounds in 1984.
9a44436f-ebfe-47a1-8da4-de554feedbf1	Sea otters	Sea otters sleep floating on their backs in groups called "rafts," often wrapping themselves in kelp and holding paws with a neighbor so they don't drift apart while asleep.
9a4596ba-796f-418d-bb17-6065584d5534	BANANA	Bananas are mildly radioactive from potassium-40 — a medium banana undergoes ~15 radioactive decays per second. Nuclear scientists informally use the "Banana Equivalent Dose" (1995, Gary Mansfield, Lawrence Livermore Lab), ~0.1 microsieverts per banana, as a plain-English radiation yardstick.
9a58bcd1-0354-4c20-9bbe-fb7885a57614	Hootie & the Blowfish	Nobody in Hootie & the Blowfish is named Hootie. Frontman Darius Rucker coined the name in 1986 by mashing together the nicknames of two college choir friends, "Hootie" for one with owl-like glasses and "Blowfish" for another with puffy cheeks.
9b4ade64-d0c5-4309-85a9-a638be2a7ad6	Skee-Ball	The original 1908 Skee-Ball alleys were 32 feet long — nearly three times today's standard 10-foot boardwalk version — with the ramp built into the middle of the lane rather than at the end, so it took real arm strength to even reach the target. Alleys were shortened after the 1929 stock market crash so operators could fit more machines into smaller spaces.
9ba39102-0663-4b19-b956-4304cd067308	Coco Chanel	In 1941 the Nazi Abwehr registered Coco Chanel as Agent F-7124, codename "Westminster." Her real mission: fly to Madrid to try to broker a secret separate peace, aimed at reaching her old friend Winston Churchill, on behalf of spy chief Wilhelm Canaris working without Hitler's knowledge.
9be57f53-1967-4ff3-9451-325944b1ff1b	Star Wars (Darth Vader)	James Earl Jones recorded all of Darth Vader's dialogue for the original 1977 Star Wars in about two and a half hours, for a fee of $7,000, and asked to go uncredited — his name didn't appear on a Star Wars film until Return of the Jedi in 1983. The man in the suit, David Prowse, never spoke a line on camera; before acting he was crowned British Heavyweight Weightlifting Champion in 1962 and held the title three years running.
9c24e7c5-40e4-4940-baae-61a9de8a1738	Sleeping Bear Dunes	In 2011, "Good Morning America" crowned this stretch of Lake Michigan dunes the "Most Beautiful Place in America," beating Cape Cod, Sedona, and Asheville in a national online vote.
9c2d338e-5c78-4261-b84f-199a7d52ae69	Potato chips	MYTH-BUST: The story that chef George Crum invented the potato chip in a huff in 1853 Saratoga — after a diner sent back his fries as too thick — is almost certainly false. Printed recipes for paper-thin fried potatoes predate him by decades, and his own sister, Catherine Wicks, claimed the credit.
9c5600d8-f9f9-4b61-8a9b-f92b9d3bc78b	SPUTNIK	The USSR's October 4, 1957 launch of Sputnik 1 — the first artificial satellite — triggered what Eisenhower called "a wave of near-hysteria" in the US, directly leading to NASA's creation in 1958 and the Defense Department's ARPA, whose ARPANET (1969) became the technical ancestor of the Internet.
9cd5bd92-3af3-4146-9fcf-c0a751d88cb8	SNICKERS	Introduced by Mars in 1930, named after the Mars family's favorite horse — not the sound of laughing.
9cdb35b8-3864-40fa-8c59-1815f82b322f	CHAUVINISM	The word comes from Nicolas Chauvin, a supposedly real French soldier said to have idolized Napoleon so blindly he kept praising him after Waterloo. No historical record of him actually existing — likely originated as an 1831 Paris stage-comedy joke character ("Je suis français, je suis Chauvin").
9cf585f7-da2e-4143-bdb9-255449dbaf68	JERRY WEST	The NBA logo (dribbling silhouette) was designed by Alan Siegel in 1969, based on a photo of Lakers guard Jerry West — never officially confirmed by the league, deliberately kept ambiguous to stay "institutional."
9d3f3e46-5215-4823-843c-f3f5e81f81fe	The 2026 FIFA World Cup	The 2026 FIFA World Cup, underway now through July 19, is the first men's World Cup ever jointly hosted by three countries — the U.S., Mexico and Canada — and the first with 48 teams instead of 32. Mexico became the first nation to host or co-host the tournament three times, having also done it in 1970 and 1986.
9d8f589d-998e-4742-b21b-7dff86510e58	Meijer Gardens (The American Horse)	A 24-foot bronze horse towers over Frederik Meijer Gardens & Sculpture Park in Grand Rapids — funded by Meijer grocery-chain founder Fred Meijer, unveiled in 1999.
9e43b415-12b7-42cb-ad91-60ded7659b98	Superman ice cream	MYTH-BUST: Michigan's blue-red-yellow Superman ice cream is often said to have been invented by Detroit's Stroh's during Prohibition, when the brewery pivoted to ice cream to survive — but that origin is unconfirmed, and the flavor predates the Superman comic (1938). Nobody can even agree what the Blue Moon component tastes like.
9e609e9a-3237-481b-98d7-dbd3d73d507b	Big League Chew	Big League Chew was invented in a minor-league bullpen. In 1977, Portland Mavericks pitcher Rob Nelson noticed teammates chewing tobacco between innings and wondered about shredding bubble gum into a pouch instead — the first batch was hand-made in 1979, and fellow Maverick (and former Yankee All-Star) Jim Bouton pitched it to the Wrigley Company as a healthier alternative for ballplayers.
9f3e96dc-74c2-47e0-abb7-6fcc29b7dc19	ELECTRIC EEL	Can discharge up to 860 volts — strongest bioelectric shock of any known animal. 1770s studies of its electric organs directly influenced Alessandro Volta, who modeled his 1800 voltaic pile (world's first true battery) on its structure.
9f6afebc-f873-4c65-b81e-f8cda35ca708	Tommy Lee Jones	He was a first-team All-Ivy offensive guard on Harvard's undefeated 1968 team and played every snap of the legendary 29–29 tie against Yale — the game the Harvard Crimson headlined "Harvard Beats Yale 29-29." His college roommate: Al Gore.
9f8cc0b2-214f-44fa-8298-64f724551582	The Laff Box (Charley Douglass)	Nearly every canned sitcom laugh you heard before the 2000s came out of one padlocked, two-foot-tall device called the Laff Box, built by sound engineer Charley Douglass. It held 320 recorded laughs on 32 tape loops, played like an organ with a keyboard and foot pedal, and Douglass guarded its inner workings as a trade secret for decades.
9f8de0eb-b35a-44f6-900d-80cdd1e3b438	Louie Louie	The FBI spent 31 months (1964–66) investigating whether the Kingsmen's "Louie Louie" hid obscene lyrics — agents played it at every speed, built a 455-page file, and concluded they were "unable to interpret any of the wording in the record." They never once talked to Jack Ely, the man who actually sang it. The slurred vocal? Ely shouted the whole thing in one take at a mic suspended near the ceiling, in a session that cost the band about $36–50.
9f9f1bca-7d27-466d-bf3a-16ad26eb73a5	Krusty Krab	The Krusty Krab isn't just a cartoon set — it's the subject of real federal case law. In 2014 a Texas company (IJR Capital Investments) tried to open actual seafood restaurants named "The Krusty Krab." Viacom sued, and in 2018 the Fifth Circuit Court of Appeals affirmed Viacom's trademark win — ruling that a fictional element from a TV show can acquire real trademark protection through the show's fame, even though "The Krusty Krab" had never been used to sell anything in the real world before.
9fcf992c-110d-496c-99a3-336049a58221	THEREMIN	The touchless instrument was invented 1920 by Russian physicist Leon Theremin. Composer Miklós Rózsa pioneered its use in Hollywood scores, including Hitchcock's *Spellbound* (1945), which won the Oscar for Best Original Score.
a08ee58d-e04b-4e8f-9e88-20de218c5425	a mushroom (honey fungus)	The largest known living organism on Earth is not a whale or a sequoia — it's a honey fungus. An Armillaria ostoyae in Oregon's Malheur National Forest sprawls about 3.4 square miles and may be ~8,000 years old. And the ORIGINAL record-holder, announced in a 1992 Nature paper, lives in Michigan's UP near Crystal Falls — a 2018 re-study with 245 DNA samples found the Michigan fungus is ~2,500 years old and ~440 tons, the mass of roughly three blue whales.

a2a48dae-0566-4fb4-88dc-d0cf0f2195db	Worcestershire sauce	Worcestershire sauce started as a batch chemists John Lea and William Perrins mixed up in 1837 trying to recreate a recipe brought back from India. It tasted so bad they abandoned the barrel in their Worcester cellar — and rediscovering it roughly 18 months later, found the now-fermented sauce was excellent.

a4fb10a1-704a-4b70-9e0d-22cba14223fb	The seventh-inning stretch	MYTH-BUST: The story that President William Howard Taft invented baseball's seventh-inning stretch in 1910 by standing up to stretch his legs — with the crowd rising out of respect, thinking he was leaving — is decades too late. A letter from 1869 already describes Cincinnati Red Stockings fans standing and stretching between halves of the seventh inning, 41 years before Taft ever attended a game.

a642fabf-a5ad-4827-a742-5dda61cc30d8	NARWHAL	Mid-1500s, a German merchant sold what he claimed was a unicorn horn to King Frederick II of Denmark — actually a narwhal tusk (the whale's left canine tooth, up to 9.8 feet). That tusk was later mounted as the central spindle of Denmark's "Unicorn Throne," used to crown Christian V in 1671, still at Rosenborg Castle.

Discussed in the British Journal of Psychiatry, not an official DSM diagnosis.

Boston dentist George Grant patented one in 1899 but never marketed it; New Jersey dentist William Lowell then made his 'Reddy Tee' a hit in the 1920s by paying golf star Walter Hagen to use it. Before tees, golfers teed off on little pyramids of wet sand.

The league later swapped the rigid steel poles for the padded flexible markers used today, in significant part because of his accident.

As recently as 6,000 years ago it was a green savanna with lakes, hippos, and giraffes before an abrupt climate shift turned it to sand within a century or two.

Americans now eat well over a billion wings on Super Bowl Sunday alone.

The Raiders then scored two touchdowns in nine seconds to win 43–32 — and nobody saw it. Furious fans blew 26 fuses on NBC's switchboard; the "Heidi Game" is why NFL broadcasts are now contractually required to air to completion in home markets.
b337c8d9-1dcb-4ab2-b233-e3a5c51f0665	Gatorade	Gatorade was invented in 1965 after University of Florida head football coach Ray Graves asked kidney specialist Dr. Robert Cade why his players lost so much weight during games and almost never needed to urinate. Cade's team found the Gators' blood sugar and electrolytes were crashing, and mixed water, sodium, sugar, potassium and lemon juice into the fix — tested on Florida players that same fall.

b3be1744-a79e-41d1-b24f-1fb81da8b3d2	Live pigeon shooting (1900 Paris Olympics)	The only Olympic event ever built around intentionally killing animals was live pigeon shooting at the 1900 Paris Games. Belgian shooter Leon de Lunden won by killing 21 birds; competitors combined to kill roughly 300 pigeons before the event was dropped forever, later replaced by clay targets in other shooting events.
b3d498c4-0ab7-41f2-b72f-b02e859b4ddf	Chef Boyardee	Chef Boyardee was a real chef — Italian immigrant Ettore "Hector" Boiardi — and a decorated WW2 supplier: when the US entered the war his company ran the factory 24/7, at peak employing ~5,000 workers and turning out 250,000 cans of rations a day for Allied troops, for which the US War Department awarded Boiardi a Gold Star order of excellence in 1946. The brand is spelled "Boy-Ar-Dee" phonetically so American customers could pronounce his name.

In 2017, Lawrence Livermore Lab and SLAC recreated the process with lasers and X-rays.

b5333eb1-bdc5-4066-b701-3ddc128b6046	Moxie	Moxie is America's oldest continuously sold soft drink — launched in 1876 as "Moxie Nerve Food," a patent medicine, then sold as a soda from 1884. Resurfacing it by hand took a five-person crew ninety minutes, so Frank Zamboni spent years designing a machine that could do it in fifteen — the first rolled out in 1949.
