b7476790-9a5a-493d-acde-8dc46e5fdba6	Salarium	MYTH (no ancient source actually says this): that Roman soldiers were literally paid in salt, and that's where "salary" comes from. The word IS real — Latin "salarium" (from "sal," salt) was a soldier's stipend, likely earmarked partly toward buying salt, which was a genuinely precious, state-controlled preservative in the ancient world. But the vivid image of legionaries being handed sacks of salt as wages appears nowhere in Roman writing — it's a modern embellishment stacked onto a real etymology.
b8362a85-a891-4552-8a9f-c7d54b4dfa58	Mickey (Toni Basil)	The cheerleader squealing "Oh Mickey, you're so fine" was a 39-year-old avant-garde choreography veteran — before her 1982 #1, Toni Basil co-directed and choreographed Talking Heads' "Once in a Lifetime" video (she set up the camera and helped Byrne stylize his preacher-in-a-trance moves), choreographed for David Bowie (Glass Spider tour), acted in Easy Rider (1969), and co-founded the pioneering street-dance crew The Lockers.
b9b3f4e6-36c3-4f0d-93cf-f0db017cb8c5	Sleeping Bear Dunes	Sleeping Bear Dunes was voted "Most Beautiful Place in America" by Good Morning America viewers in 2011, beating out nine other national nominees. The dunes' name predates that vote by centuries: an Ojibwe/Chippewa legend holds that a mother bear and her two cubs swam across Lake Michigan fleeing a forest fire; the cubs drowned short of shore, and the Great Spirit raised North and South Manitou Island to mark where they went under while the grieving mother bear waits, buried in the dune, on the mainland shore.
b9ed9d25-da79-4155-afbf-31d092c39366	Ketchup	Ketchup started life with no tomatoes at all — the word comes from Hokkien kê-chiap, a fermented fish brine from southern China, and Brits made it with mushrooms from the 1600s on. Tomato ketchup is American (first recipe 1812, by James Mease) — and in the 1830s Dr. John Cook Bennett declared tomatoes a cure for indigestion, jaundice, and diarrhea, boiled ketchup down and sold it in pharmacies as tomato PILLS; the craze died around 1850 after copycats got caught selling laxatives (some with no tomato in them) as tomato pills.
ba1d0773-bae2-40ee-aa86-f4cf8a32a1b2	Rubber Duck	The rubber duck was inducted into the National Toy Hall of Fame in 2013 (alongside chess), decades after 28,800 of them — part of a "Friendly Floatees" bath-toy shipment — spilled off a cargo ship in the North Pacific in January 1992. Oceanographer Curtis Ebbesmeyer tracked their landfalls for years and, with colleague James Ingraham, used the data to build an ocean-current model (OSCAR) that correctly predicted where they'd wash up next — some turned up on UK beaches over a decade later.
ba8d5188-751f-40f9-b116-94cc77dd26c9	Neil Diamond ("Sweet Caroline")	Neil Diamond wrote "Sweet Caroline" in 1969 after seeing a magazine photo of nine-year-old Caroline Kennedy, JFK's daughter, dressed in riding gear beside her pony. He kept the inspiration secret for almost 40 years, finally telling her himself when he performed the song via satellite at her 50th birthday party. Today the song is best known as Fenway Park's eighth-inning singalong tradition for the Boston Red Sox.
bace782a-aa46-496f-8c93-b28d883bc6e6	TANGO	"Tango" is the word for "T" in the NATO phonetic alphabet, finalized 1956 after linguist Jean-Paul Vinay tested code words across 31 nationalities to avoid confusion with "Bravo," "Delta," "Papa," "Victor" over garbled radio.
bad58680-afca-4993-b388-7a3574a86d21	Chuck Berry	"Johnny B. Goode" is the only rock and roll song on the Voyager Golden Record, launched aboard NASA's Voyager 1 and 2 in 1977 as humanity's musical calling card to the cosmos (Carl Sagan had final say on the track list).
bb1109d3-fae9-4408-8593-552444dace11	Law & Order	The "dun dun" is a copyrighted piece of music titled "The Clang," built by composer Mike Post from sampled sounds including a slamming jail door, a hammer on an anvil, and — per Post — 500 Japanese men stamping their feet on a wooden floor. Because it's registered as music rather than a sound effect, Post collects a royalty every time it plays.
bc2ad983-27e0-4923-b11f-9bc262090d37	A Christmas Story	The story is set in fictional Hohman, Indiana, based on author Jean Shepherd's real hometown of Hammond, Indiana — but director Bob Clark shot the exteriors in Cleveland, Ohio, because of its real Higbee's department store, which became the film's iconic Santa-line set.
bc2e9c1d-e201-4461-8dc9-82ca03e5ac98	Ghostbusters	Ray Parker Jr.'s title theme was built to fit a temp-track scene the studio sent him — one scored to Huey Lewis and the News' "I Want a New Drug," a song Lewis himself had already turned down writing the film's theme around. After the movie became a hit, Lewis sued Parker for lifting the melody and bassline; the two settled under an NDA that Lewis later broke on VH1's Behind the Music, and Parker then successfully countersued him for breach.
bc528d30-ec04-4c3b-bf32-a4b29edae320	I Will Survive	The Grammys created the "Best Disco Recording" category for the 1980 ceremony, right as the anti-disco backlash was peaking months after Disco Demolition Night. "I Will Survive" won it, and the category was killed the very next year - the first and only Grammy ever handed out for disco.
bc999375-21d2-4040-ba92-b1d6f7128c84	Friends	The show's theme was originally under a minute long for the opening credits; a Nashville radio program director looped it into a full song, requests exploded, and Warner Bros. forced the Rembrandts to record a complete 3-minute version (May 1995) — which then topped Billboard's Hot 100 Airplay chart for 8 weeks without even being available as a retail single, a first.
bcb01ecf-9bc7-4763-8630-c900ba7af1ec	"CANDLE IN THE WIND 1997" (Elton John)	His rewrite for Princess Diana's funeral is the 2nd-best-selling physical single of all time worldwide (behind only Bing Crosby's "White Christmas") — 33 million copies, best-selling single since pop charts began in the 1950s.
bcb9af04-bec3-4618-b87e-146b72eb7037	Isle Royale	This Michigan national park sits in Lake Superior 53 miles from the Michigan mainland but only 15 miles from Ontario — closer to Canada than to the state that owns it. It's also the least-visited national park in the lower 48 (~17,000-29,000 visitors/year, boat or seaplane only).
bd73b24a-6cb7-4ebf-a333-e629208a5a8d	Ouija board	The Ouija board got its U.S. patent in 1891 only after a skeptical patent officer demanded proof: he told the inventors that if the board could spell out his name — which he had not given them — they would get the patent. It reportedly did. The board itself supposedly chose the name 'Ouija,' claiming it meant 'good luck.'
bd88adaf-6bd8-44ec-818a-56e6bd294d44	Red Bull	Red Bull is a reworked version of a Thai energy drink called Krating Daeng ("red gaur"), sold to Thai truck drivers by Chaleo Yoovidhya. Austrian salesman Dietrich Mateschitz tried it for jet lag in 1982, then partnered with Yoovidhya in 1984 — each put in $500,000 — to carbonate and de-sweeten it for European tastes.
bd8d4127-ea2a-458f-ac49-9ef005e7b961	Groundhog Day	The 1993 Bill Murray film is set in Punxsutawney, PA, but not a single frame was shot there — it was filmed in Woodstock, Illinois, 61 miles northwest of Chicago. Crews built a fake "Welcome to Punxsutawney" sign and removed it after filming.
be73dfee-b51a-4e1b-b468-e89b757c210b	Hail Mary	Roger Staubach didn't invent the "Hail Mary" pass with the famous 1975 Cowboys-Vikings touchdown — he'd already used the phrase 12 years earlier as a Navy quarterback, describing a desperation touchdown against Michigan in 1963. The term goes back further still: Notre Dame players literally said a Hail Mary prayer in the huddle before a game-winning score against Georgia Tech in 1922.
bfd4d2db-43a0-4985-8627-feb52e52c2b6	GIRAFFE	Blue-black tongue (likely sunburn protection), up to 18-20 inches long. Despite a 6-foot neck, exactly 7 neck vertebrae — same count as a human — just wildly elongated (11+ inches each vs. our ~1 inch).
c12e6709-7a28-4861-8818-550a7ffac3ba	VAMPIRE SQUID	Despite the name, it's neither a true squid nor octopus — sole survivor of its own ancient order — and doesn't drink blood; it's a deep-sea detritivore eating marine snow.
c282bbe4-e522-403b-bcb5-a0858da67878	GREEN BAY PACKERS	Only publicly owned, non-profit team in major US pro sports — 537,000+ shareholders, no one allowed to own more than a small fraction. Dates to 1923, when local businessmen ("Hungry Five") sold stock to save the team from insolvency; grandfathered against the NFL's current single-controlling-owner rule.
c3f8f12a-61db-43d6-83ff-82c6107aed79	Prince	In 1993, amid a contract dispute with Warner Bros., Prince legally changed his stage name to an unpronounceable glyph and was known for years as "The Artist Formerly Known As Prince."
c430d8d5-4fce-4e0b-8349-89037a5a8a37	Toy Story	The license plate on Andy's mom's minivan reads "A113" — the classroom number for first-year Character Animation students at CalArts, where John Lasseter, Brad Bird, Pete Docter, and Tim Burton all studied.
c488fcdb-60e8-4ce6-b23e-82abe94167c8	Edward Lowe (Kitty Litter)	Kitty Litter was invented by accident in Cassopolis, Michigan in 1947, when Edward Lowe gave a neighbor a bag of absorbent clay — fuller's earth, meant for soaking up factory grease spills — instead of the sand she'd asked for to fill her cat's box. She loved it so much he started bagging the clay and driving it door to door; the company he founded later created Tidy Cat.
c53de5d6-4f49-43c7-b244-7dde2cc7d7d4	Nathan's Hot Dog Eating Contest	MYTH-BUST: Nathan's Famous Hot Dog Eating Contest was not founded in 1916 by four immigrants settling an argument over patriotism. That origin story was invented in the 1970s by publicist Morty Matz as a PR stunt. The earliest documented contest didn't happen until 1972, and the tradition only exploded after Japan's Takeru Kobayashi nearly doubled the winning total in 2001.
c594befc-91b8-42a0-956f-d0b477a9fb5f	THE GREAT CHICAGO FIRE	Oct. 8-9, 1871, killed ~300 people, destroyed 17,000+ buildings across ~2,100 acres.
c5c3e213-253e-4ccf-a839-929402ad9153	Liberia	The 1982 Guinness Book of Records lists the most fraudulent election in world history: Liberia's 1927 presidential vote, where incumbent Charles D. B. King officially received ~234,000 votes (sources range 234k–240k) in a country with roughly 15,000 registered voters — more than 15 times the possible turnout. His challenger's fraud accusations spiraled into a League of Nations slavery investigation that forced King to resign in 1930.
c5d52f17-87b0-4ae1-aa25-1a4232d2981e	The Shining	In Stephen King's novel the Grady sisters aren't twins, but Kubrick recast them that way after his assistant Leon Vitali showed him Diane Arbus's 1967 photograph "Identical Twins, Roselle, New Jersey" from a 1972 MoMA retrospective catalog. Kubrick had a personal connection to the image — Arbus had mentored him in the late 1940s when he was a rookie photographer for Look magazine.
c74a7861-cb26-4fd7-bad9-90d91f0f9601	Abbey Road	MYTH-BUST: The Abbey Road crossing is NOT a UNESCO World Heritage site — it's a Grade II listed structure under English Heritage (December 2010), the first zebra crossing ever listed. The listing also credits the crossing type's inventor: Lord Hore-Belisha, namesake of the 'Belisha beacon.'
c79377ad-55e6-4a10-83f2-6aad592cfa26	DUNG BEETLE	*Scarabaeus satyrus* is the only insect confirmed to navigate by the Milky Way — a 2013 Lund University study (*Current Biology*) showed they roll dung balls in straight lines using the galaxy's light band, even on moonless nights.
c814d1c5-4a09-4e96-bcd4-d4a6392138e8	Ferris Bueller's Day Off	None of the three "Ferraris" destroyed and driven on-screen were real — all three were fiberglass replicas of a Ferrari 250 GT California built by Modena Design & Development; Ferrari sued Modena for trademark infringement over the badges.
c86fbcc1-bb7e-44e6-b0ce-9467a545049c	Mentos	Dropping Mentos into Diet Coke doesn't cause a chemical reaction — it's pure physics. A 2008 peer-reviewed study by Appalachian State physicist Tonya Coffey found the candy's pitted, rough surface (and a gum arabic coating that dissolves fast) creates thousands of nucleation sites that let dissolved CO2 escape almost instantly, launching a geyser.
c8be04d2-1c52-4f3e-af1b-aabbffb1ad30	Spaghetti	Richard Feynman spent an entire evening in his kitchen with fellow physicist Danny Hillis trying to break a strand of dry spaghetti into exactly two pieces by bending it — it kept snapping into three or more, and by the end the kitchen floor was covered in broken pasta and they still didn't know why. The 2006 Ig Nobel Prize in Physics went to French physicists Basile Audoly and Sebastien Neukirch for finally explaining it: the initial break triggers a "snap-back" vibration wave that shatters the rest of the strand.
c95eafe7-c9d0-4572-bfcf-8979106f4a89	Sherlock Holmes	In April 2016, a sonar sweep of Loch Ness (Kongsberg's Munin underwater robot, "Operation Groundtruth") finally found a monster — a 30-foot Nessie prop 180 meters down, built for Billy Wilder's 1970 film The Private Life of Sherlock Holmes. It sank during filming in 1969 because Wilder ordered its two humps removed over crew warnings that the humps were what kept it afloat.
cb6dad7e-ad0b-4a22-832f-32c0bbf7767a	The Jersey Devil	During the January 1909 sighting panic — hundreds of reports in one week — South Jersey schools closed, mills sent workers home, and the Philadelphia Zoo posted a $10,000 reward for its capture, which drew hoaxers including a kangaroo fitted with fake wings. The reward has never been claimed.
cb6e9021-f020-43a6-8cb8-34d6a6779cc0	Jacuzzi	Jacuzzi was a family of Italian-immigrant aviation engineers — they built propellers for WWI aircraft — before a child's illness changed the business. In the 1940s Candido Jacuzzi adapted one of the family's agricultural water pumps into a submersible bath jet to ease his young son's rheumatoid arthritis. The hot tub grew out of a medical device.
cbb86c4a-73ff-4e36-bdf0-a7078b7901bb	GIANT SQUID	No one saw a live giant squid in its natural habitat until Sept. 30, 2004, when Japanese researcher Tsunemi Kubodera got the first photographs; first video didn't come until July 2012.
cbc0bf49-951c-4bb2-b5fa-a2f651d5db68	WOMBAT	Only known animal to produce cube-shaped droppings (up to 80-100 cubes/night) — uneven intestinal stiffness makes them stack instead of roll. Won the 2019 Ig Nobel Prize in Physics.
cc05500a-1fd2-471a-ad96-8fcf88ec240e	Hummer (the cocktail)	The Hummer — white rum, Kahlúa, and two scoops of vanilla ice cream, so thick a straw stands upright, with nearly 4 oz of booze in a 7.5-oz glass — was invented in 1968 at Detroit's Bayview Yacht Club by bartender Jerome Adams, and is effectively Michigan's signature cocktail. It got its name when the club's bar chairman Ed Jacoby downed a few and declared "After two of these damn things, kinda makes you wanna hum." (A 1982 Detroit Free Press piece once credited the London Chop House instead — either way, it's Detroit's drink.)
ce891e84-bce2-4779-8c23-1ee11cba0ea0	Seinfeld	Cosmo Kramer is based on Larry David's real across-the-hall neighbor Kenny Kramer, who has run official "Kramer's Reality Tour" bus tours of NYC Seinfeld filming spots for decades (parodied in-show in "The Muffin Tops").
cf8aba18-d985-4780-8c08-21f9b9563422	Rogue Wave	For centuries "walls of water" that appear suddenly out of normal seas were dismissed by scientists as sailors' tall tales — physically impossible under standard wave models. That changed on New Year's Day 1995, when a laser sensor on Norway's Draupner oil platform recorded an 84-foot wave rising out of 39-foot seas, the first rogue wave ever measured by an instrument instead of just witnessed.
cfeb8ab6-cfb5-4768-8c9a-1e1eda4e7bae	THE ROSETTA STONE	Discovered near Rosetta, Egypt in 1799; its parallel Greek/hieroglyphic/demotic text let Champollion crack Egyptian hieroglyphics in 1822, after 1,400+ years unreadable.
d038e95b-df4c-4a42-bc02-6ab4017cddf4	Bigfoot	The FBI really has a Bigfoot file — 22 pages, declassified June 2019. In 1976 Peter Byrne of the Bigfoot Information Center in The Dalles, Oregon, mailed the Bureau ~15 hairs attached to a scrap of skin; the FBI lab broke its own evidence-only policy to analyze them and concluded in 1977: "the hairs are of deer family origin." The kicker: the reply apparently never reached Byrne — he learned his Bigfoot hair was deer 43 years later, at age 93, when reporters called about the released file.
d097d7c2-0fd3-4429-b711-ee12c00e6f26	Faygo	Faygo started in Detroit in 1907 as Feigenson Brothers Bottling Works — Russian immigrant bakers Ben and Perry Feigenson turned their cake-frosting recipes into soda flavors (grape, strawberry, fruit punch). They shortened 'Feigenson' to 'Faygo' in the 1920s so it would fit on a bottle.
d0bf7c33-f4bd-4c81-b161-8babbbde8f4b	The Cranberries	The "Zombie" band started as "The Cranberry Saw Us" — a Limerick pun that, said fast, sounds exactly like "cranberry sauce." A returned demo tape came back in the mail addressed simply to "The Cranberries," and the shortened name stuck — before Dolores O'Riordan had even joined as singer.
d12a46f0-c152-4828-b308-6a907ebb20a5	WILLY WONKA & THE CHOCOLATE FACTORY	Dahl's novel is *Charlie and the Chocolate Factory* — the studio renamed the 1971 film after the side character specifically to promote a real candy bar; Quaker Oats financed it through a candy subsidiary (later renamed The Willy Wonka Candy Company).
d12fce74-9efb-4ced-a463-9625226769d9	Log Cabin Syrup	The syrup's log-cabin-shaped bottle is a tribute — grocer Patrick J. Towle named the brand in 1887 after his childhood hero Abraham Lincoln, honoring the log cabin where Lincoln was born.
d2948770-c031-4e65-8b2b-3050ecd0f3df	PET ROCK	In 1975, ad copywriter Gary Dahl sold ordinary Rosarito, Mexico beach stones as "Pet Rocks" with a tongue-in-cheek 36-page training manual — moving ~1.3-1.5 million units in six months, making him a millionaire.
d3b080ad-0721-4281-acfc-a2953a60bee0	Q	Not a single U.S. state name contains the letter Q — the only letter in the alphabet with that distinction. Every other letter shows up somewhere, even J and Z, thanks to New Jersey and Arizona.
d49b3fa0-1a02-41d3-9bea-87962ac2ac70	Nauru	This tiny Pacific island — the world's third-smallest country by area — got rich almost overnight in the 1970s by strip-mining its own ground into cash: its entire interior turned out to be nearly pure phosphate, formed over millennia from seabird droppings. By 1975 it had the second-highest per capita GDP on Earth (behind only Saudi Arabia); by the 2000s the phosphate was gone, 80% of the island was an unlivable moonscape, and the country was nearly bankrupt.
d513d6e6-957c-4e5f-8475-8f00b1a2fb4f	LEGO	LEGO holds the Guinness World Record as the world's largest tire manufacturer by units — roughly 300+ million tiny rubber tires a year, peaking at 381 million in 2010. Nearly half of all LEGO sets include wheels.
d51c197d-8e1a-434a-9263-a6bbd61bcdb5	William Howard Taft	"Taft got stuck in the White House bathtub" — no contemporary account, official record, or newspaper story supports it ever happening. Historian Alexis Coe found zero evidence; the tale traces to a 1929 magazine piece and chief usher Ike Hoover's 1934 memoir, both decades after the fact. What IS true: a custom tub so enormous a February 1909 Engineering Review photo shows four grown men sitting in it comfortably.
d595a663-529e-4305-b921-05439313fb74	Bill Post (Pop-Tarts)	The Pop-Tart was developed in 1963-64 by Bill Post, a Grand Rapids, Michigan native who was plant manager at Keebler's predecessor, Hekman Biscuit Co., when Kellogg's approached him with the concept. Post's team turned the idea into a shelf-stable toaster pastry in about four months; Post himself had started at the company at age 16, washing trucks.
d5b1c437-f939-4466-9b82-0ed5da3b7e79	Come On Eileen (Dexys Midnight Runners)	The band name comes from Dexedrine — "Dexys" — the amphetamine Northern Soul dancers took to dance all night, hence "Midnight Runners." And its one week at US No. 1 (April 23, 1983) landed exactly between "Billie Jean" and "Beat It," making it the song that denied Michael Jackson back-to-back No. 1s at his Thriller peak.
d5c35fcf-2403-4e69-af47-955c9f75275a	NOSTALGIA	Coined 1688 by Swiss doctor Johannes Hofer from Greek *nostos* ("homecoming") + *algos* ("pain") — invented to diagnose a real, sometimes-fatal condition afflicting Swiss mercenaries abroad.
d5feb919-1014-4f48-a92f-7db1d8c586d6	Kellogg's corn flakes (Battle Creek, Michigan)	Corn flakes were invented as a bland health food meant to curb 'passions' for patients at a Seventh-day Adventist sanitarium in Battle Creek, Michigan. Brothers John Harvey and Will Keith Kellogg developed the recipe together in the 1890s — then fell out so badly over how to market it that they ended up suing each other.
d637fc13-f139-4df7-a337-f94d10c139d4	Oumuamua	In 2017, a Canadian astronomer using a Hawaiian telescope spotted the first confirmed object from outside our solar system ever detected passing through it — cigar-or-pancake-shaped, tumbling, reddish, and already on its way back out by the time anyone saw it. It was named using Hawaiian language for "scout" or "messenger reaching out from the distant past."
d6d22f66-b9ca-496d-acc0-091549b4bdaa	ALEXANDER GRAHAM BELL	Won the US telephone patent by mere hours over rival Elisha Gray on Feb. 14, 1876 — Bell filed a full application that morning, Gray a lesser "caveat" hours later, still debated by historians.
d74aa436-af46-435f-b9bb-4f16d4c074b1	DETROIT LIONS	Have hosted an NFL Thanksgiving Day game every year since 1934 (skipping only WWII years) — started by owner George A. Richards, who also owned radio station WJR and got the game aired across NBC's whole network.
d78b1a50-6f1c-4164-a63e-3310d7b0a0ce	ELLIS ISLAND	~12 million immigrants were processed there between 1892 and 1954 — an estimated two-fifths of living Americans can trace ancestry through it.
d79483e3-3974-4d5f-8abf-78ac37b5e11a	Guinness World Records (born from a beer executive's pub argument)	The Guinness Book of Records exists because Guinness's own managing director, Sir Hugh Beaver, got into an argument at a 1951 shooting party over which was Europe's fastest game bird and couldn't find a reference book to settle it. He commissioned twin researchers to compile a book of facts as a pub-argument-settling giveaway; it published in 1955 and became its own industry.
d843ec6c-e323-455b-a5e7-9185021a067f	KILIMANJARO	Tallest free-standing mountain on Earth, not part of any range. Its ice cap — the one that inspired Hemingway's *The Snows of Kilimanjaro* — has lost 80%+ of its coverage since 1912, projected to vanish by mid-century.
d85904ab-49af-40cd-b3ee-1c9493769227	Terry Crews	Before Old Spice ads or Brooklyn Nine-Nine, Terry Crews spent six NFL seasons as a linebacker/defensive end — drafted by the Rams in 1991, then the Packers, Chargers, an NFL Europe team in Germany, the Redskins, and the Eagles — before retiring in 1997 to chase acting.
d8a66ea6-f924-4dd2-8ad0-8ce3f1c59a39	Tahquamenon Falls	Michigan's Upper Peninsula waterfall is the second-largest east of the Mississippi — 200 feet wide, 48 feet tall — and its water runs root-beer colored from tannic acid leached out of upstream cedar/hemlock swamps.
d8b85b0b-0e77-4b4c-82f0-22c1ea58db68	CHICHEN ITZA	The El Castillo pyramid is a temple to Kukulkan, the Maya feathered-serpent god — on the spring/fall equinoxes, shadows on its staircase form the illusion of a serpent slithering down the steps.
d8dc0c3a-8a48-41ef-89e1-8abd1cc5092c	Sigourney Weaver	Her reported $11 million salary (plus co-producer credit) for Alien Resurrection (1997) was as much as the entire production budget of the original Alien (1979, budget commonly cited at $11M; Wikipedia gives $8.4–14M) — a film she made for roughly $33,000.
d90b3ebb-5331-4667-b35f-baf7e4392df0	Islands in the Stream	The signature Kenny Rogers–Dolly Parton country duet (a 1983 pop #1) was written entirely by the Bee Gees, originally in an R&B style intended for Marvin Gaye, and its title comes from a posthumous Ernest Hemingway novel.
d9181cac-e2c2-4389-805a-5540b5f85d73	Cherenkov Radiation	The eerie blue glow around a nuclear reactor core isn't radioactivity itself — it's Cherenkov radiation, a light shockwave from charged particles moving through water faster than light travels in that water. Pavel Cherenkov shared the 1958 Nobel Prize in Physics for explaining it.
d92e5209-8b95-43bd-a813-393d3bba0b5b	SARCASM	From Greek *sarkazein*, "to strip off the flesh, like dogs" — from *sarx* ("flesh").
d990567c-b7a5-4f9b-a297-d094bbd07f5e	Wrigley	Wrigley chewing gum began as a free giveaway. In 1891 William Wrigley Jr. sold soap, then baking powder, throwing in free gum as a bonus — when the gum outsold the actual product, he switched businesses. That pivot eventually paid for the Chicago Cubs' ballpark, still named Wrigley Field.
d9983ec8-8f21-4e67-bed3-ee3657db4d96	Techno (the Belleville Three)	Techno music was born not in Berlin or Chicago but in Belleville, Michigan — a small town outside Detroit — where three high-school friends, Juan Atkins, Derrick May and Kevin Saunderson (the 'Belleville Three'), fused Kraftwerk and funk in the early 1980s. Atkins is called the Godfather of Techno.
d9ec006f-b8ef-4fd8-b1eb-0ba05fdcb868	Pitcairn Islands	This British territory in the South Pacific has a permanent population of just 35 people (as of the 2023 count) — the smallest population of any political jurisdiction on Earth — nearly all of them descended from just nine mutineers off HMS Bounty and the twelve Tahitian women who fled with them in 1790.
da3b9cbd-a7bf-4df4-883c-e7d0f22287e6	Thomas Edison	The oldest recording of a human voice beat his phonograph by 17 years — on April 9, 1860, Frenchman Édouard-Léon Scott de Martinville's phonautograph traced 20 seconds of "Au Clair de la Lune" into soot-covered paper. The machine could record but not play back, so nobody heard it until 2008, when Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory scientists optically scanned the squiggles into sound. First playback ran at double speed — the world thought it was a woman or girl singing until researchers corrected it to a man, almost certainly Scott himself, singing very slowly.
da6e2195-d40c-40c3-b991-59db12a59c10	Carl Sagan	In 1958 the US Air Force ran classified Project A119 — a plan to detonate a W25 nuclear warhead (1.7 kt) on the Moon's terminator line as a visible Cold War show of force. A young Carl Sagan, then a doctoral student, did the math on how the dust cloud would expand in vacuum so the flash could be seen from Earth. The project stayed secret for 40+ years — and only surfaced because Sagan himself leaked classified paper titles on a 1959 Berkeley fellowship application, which biographer Keay Davidson found in the 1990s; project leader Leonard Reiffel then confirmed everything in 2000.
da7a4570-a949-409d-9c49-04a31d9f7290	(Arizona) Cardinals	The NFL's oldest franchise got its name from laundry-grade thrift: in 1901, Chicago club owner Chris O'Brien bought used jerseys off the University of Chicago, whose maroon had faded so badly he declared "That's not maroon, it's cardinal red!" — and renamed his team the Cardinals. The team traces to the 1898 Morgan Athletic Club, making it the oldest continuously operating professional football team in the US.
dabf944b-68e0-4d0b-80d3-1d6feeb097f2	Dolly Parton	Died August 25, 2026, at 80 in Nashville (confirmed: NPR/NBC/Variety/CNN obituaries). Deep cut for the tribute slot: she wrote "9 to 5" on the movie set using her acrylic fingernails as a rhythm instrument — clicking them to mimic a typewriter — and the nails play on the actual recording. The 9 to 5 and Odd Jobs album carries the back-cover credit "Nails by Dolly."
db256c03-f66d-485c-9140-c3ea3d33b933	Cardigan	The button-front knit sweater is named for James Thomas Brudenell, 7th Earl of Cardigan, who led the doomed Charge of the Light Brigade at the Battle of Balaclava (Crimean War, 1854) — he reportedly wore one leading the charge; the word entered English by 1868.
db52372d-961d-4d72-9f1f-32cfe5265644	Lancer	JFK's Secret Service codename was "Lancer." The Kennedy presidency was never called "Camelot" while he was alive — Jacqueline Kennedy created that framing days after the assassination, in a December 1963 Life interview with Theodore H. White, quoting the closing lyrics of the musical Camelot.
dcc01450-5269-45fd-85e2-bf519bc7026d	McDonald's	The world's only "float-through" McDonald's — a drive-thru for kayaks and canoes, order via app, staff hand your bag down to a dock — sits on a canal off the Elbe River in Hamburg, Germany.
dd0f11da-de06-406d-819b-e1b3c158eab4	Cheap Trick	Cheap Trick's name isn't a lyric: bassist Tom Petersson coined it in 1973 watching a Slade concert, muttering that the glam-rock headliners were pulling "every cheap trick in the book" onstage.
dd329f81-d95c-4e55-a9ed-36f3c8ad6ac8	Duracell	The pink drumming battery bunny didn't start as Energizer's mascot - Duracell created it in 1973. Duracell let the US trademark lapse by 1988, and Energizer swooped in with its own version; a 1992 settlement split the rights territorially, with Duracell still owning the bunny everywhere outside the US and Canada.
de190dde-54d7-42d7-975b-3ed922303a14	Mackinac Island fudge	Mackinac Island's signature fudge started as a marketing gimmick. When the Murdick family opened a candy shop there in the 1880s, son Rome Murdick began pouring the mixture onto marble slabs and working it by hand in the shop window as a public show — the theatrics, not just the taste, are why the island now has more than a dozen fudge shops and reportedly imports roughly 10 tons of sugar a week. Tourists are still nicknamed 'fudgies.'
def35ae0-8ab7-4310-bb9a-bbf9ba84d514	The America's Cup	The New York Yacht Club held it for 132 straight years (1851–1983) — 25 straight defenses, the longest winning streak in sports history — until Australia II and its secret winged keel came back from 3–1 down in 1983. And the trophy isn't named for the country: it's named after the schooner America that won the first race around the Isle of Wight in 1851.
e08a1384-f5ef-4a7f-9032-8990ad113adb	The Disneyland Monorail	At its June 14, 1959 dedication — America's first daily-operating monorail — designer Bob Gurr piloted it with Walt Disney, VP Richard Nixon, and Nixon's daughters aboard. When the girls begged "Again!", Walt told Gurr to take a second unscheduled lap, leaving Nixon's entire Secret Service detail sprinting down the platform after a departing Vice President. Gurr later admitted the train had barely been tested and he feared it might catch fire with the Second Family aboard.
e11f6040-c975-4be8-bd13-0dc33146f58d	DEAD SEA	At ~440m below sea level, its shore is the lowest exposed land on Earth — dropping ~1m/year, shrunk from 1,050 km² (1930) to 605 km² today, spawning 6,000+ sinkholes since the 1980s. Water is 9.6x saltier than the ocean (why swimmers float effortlessly); traditionally tied to biblical Sodom and Gomorrah.
e18e5efa-fdeb-4de8-bf94-1bd6bed113df	SWEET CAROLINE (Neil Diamond)	The Red Sox have played it mid-8th-inning at every Fenway home game since 2002.
e28c461f-daeb-4f5c-b5b2-65a83a7d179e	Jenga	Jenga's name comes from the Swahili word 'kujenga,' meaning 'to build.' Inventor Leslie Scott grew up in East Africa playing a homemade version with sawmill scrap blocks her family collected, then self-published the game in England in 1983 after borrowing 15,000 pounds from a bank — Hasbro didn't pick it up until three years later.
e336190a-4ab8-40cb-921d-9caddf1d65f9	ROBOCOP	The 1987 *RoboCop* — entirely about a dystopian Detroit — wasn't filmed in Detroit. Verhoeven shot it in Dallas, TX, because Dallas had the right mix of decaying blocks next to skyscrapers to sell "Old Detroit."
e36020bc-72a7-478d-8696-4e1c48cac360	Pittsburgh Steelers	The Pittsburgh Steelers are the only NFL team with their logo on just one side of the helmet — not a design choice, an oversight. When owner Dan Rooney had the logo added in 1962, he told the equipment manager to put it on the right side and never specified both, so it only ever went on one.
e460e06f-44f3-4219-823f-db93d3b6b332	HUMMINGBIRD	Only bird capable of sustained backward flight (uniquely mobile shoulder joint, ~180° wing rotation). Can drop into overnight torpor — heart rate from 1,000+ bpm to under 50.
e46ff026-9f3d-4854-a22e-f4e636373686	Hat trick (cricket, H.H. Stephenson, 1858)	A 'hat trick' is literally about a hat. In 1858, English cricketer H.H. Stephenson took three wickets on three consecutive balls, and grateful fans passed a hat around to buy him one as a reward. The phrase later spread from cricket into hockey, soccer, and beyond.
e4885abd-9c7e-47d5-b704-c7a6ec765916	TROJAN HORSE	In Greek legend, Odysseus's hollow wooden horse let Greek soldiers sneak inside Troy's walls after a ten-year siege the Greeks couldn't win by force.
e52120e3-c40e-41cc-b00b-7379ebc4b911	GUSTAVE EIFFEL	After finishing his tower for the 1889 World's Fair, Eiffel built a private apartment at the top (piano, paisley wallpaper) he rarely let guests into. One exception: Thomas Edison, who visited Sept. 1889 and gifted him a phonograph; Eiffel's recorded voice survives in the French National Library.
e5cc56de-f85a-4f50-bc22-1cb5abfb6c14	ZOOTOPIA	For most of development, con-artist fox Nick Wilde was the film's lead and rookie rabbit cop Judy Hopps was his sidekick — the filmmakers were "very committed" to that version until a November 2014 story overhaul, well into production, flipped the leads.
e5db593b-918f-4e6f-9c4f-f56a4b627cc5	Tim Duncan	He wasn't a basketball prodigy — he was a teenage swimming standout in the 50/100/400m freestyle in St. Croix, aiming for the 1992 Olympics, until Hurricane Hugo (September 1989) destroyed the island's only Olympic-size pool. Forced into open-ocean training, his fear of sharks killed his enthusiasm, and he didn't pick up basketball until high school — then won five NBA titles with the Spurs.
e63b1d6e-87e7-40f6-8768-c89853038491	Leotard	The leotard is named after Jules Léotard, the French aerialist who INVENTED the flying trapeze — first routine performed November 12, 1859 at the Cirque Napoleon in Paris, after a childhood spent practicing over the swimming pool his father ran in Toulouse. He called his skin-tight one-piece a "maillot"; the garment only took his name after his death (he died in 1870 at just 32, likely of smallpox).
e6c812df-6a64-43c8-b2c6-2b1f27d15657	Queen Elizabeth II	When she visited the US, the Secret Service assigned her the code name "Kittyhawk" (per agent Joseph Petro, who protected her 1983 visit; the official code-name list also records "Redfern") — the same name as the North Carolina town where the Wright brothers first flew.
e76b0c5e-9bc7-445f-8bdd-f6e1e39693f7	BLACK SABBATH	Originally called Earth, the band saw a line of people across the street queued for the 1963 horror film *Black Sabbath* (dir. Mario Bava, starring Boris Karloff) — renamed themselves after it and wrote a matching song.
e7a1eddf-1999-469d-adfb-f930eaf48fd6	The traffic light (William Potts, Detroit)	The modern three-color traffic light was invented by Detroit police officer William Potts in 1920. Earlier signals were only red and green; Potts added the amber 'caution' light using railroad lamps and about $37 of wire, and built the first four-way tri-color signal for the corner of Woodward and Michigan Avenues.
e7a3cd68-a8bf-4863-83a8-2fb3b9257934	LINKIN PARK	Originally Xero, then Hybrid Theory, renamed after Lincoln Park in Santa Monica (near Chester Bennington) — deliberately misspelled "Linkin" to get the domain.
e7bb20b9-b6e2-48ba-9957-3e40d605d9d6	Chumbawamba	In 2002 General Motors paid the anarchist collective behind "Tubthumping" a reported $70,000–$100,000 (sources give both figures) to use their song "Pass It Along" in a Pontiac Vibe TV ad. The band took the check, then donated every dollar to two anti-corporate groups, IndyMedia and CorpWatch — which used GM's own money to fund campaigns against GM. They had earlier turned down $1.5M from Nike for "Tubthumping" in about 30 seconds.
e7d4398d-6461-4c53-9a82-76abc6ab8250	Shampoo	"Shampoo" entered English in 1762 from Hindi "champo" ("press, knead the muscles") — for its first century it meant a full-body massage, not hair washing (that sense didn't appear until around 1860).
e7eaba4e-5633-48ad-ae31-15f2a6594ae6	Home Alone	The real Georgian house used as the McCallister home sits at 671 Lincoln Avenue in Winnetka, Illinois — a real North Shore Chicago suburb — and sold for $5.5 million in January 2025, about $250,000 over its asking price, after only a week on the market.
e837eabf-d674-4fa0-b3c5-ae53cbf4886a	Charles Durning	The beloved comic character actor from Tootsie and Dog Day Afternoon was a decorated WWII combat veteran, landing with the 1st Infantry Division at Normandy, wounded by a mine, later fighting through the Battle of the Bulge, and coming home with a Silver Star and three Purple Hearts.
e8685014-c6c1-46ff-b10b-1bc47f0f93dc	BARBIE	Mattel's Ruth Handler invented Barbie after watching her daughter Barbara act out grown-up fantasies with paper dolls, naming the doll after her. Debuted at Toy Fair March 9, 1959, for $3, selling 300,000 in year one.
e886c499-82fd-4525-92d2-a3cab0e6c5e1	PONYTAIL	The 2012 Ig Nobel Prize in Physics went to Cambridge mathematician Raymond Goldstein and colleagues for a differential equation predicting the shape and swing of a human ponytail from hair elasticity, curliness, and gravity alone.
e904ca35-e1bf-4981-beff-413d77f5d51f	Reno	Despite Nevada sitting east of California, Reno (119°49′W) is actually farther west than Los Angeles (118°14′W) — the California coastline bends southeast enough that LA ends up east of it.
e9312efe-c9d7-46b8-87ca-fce29a40146d	SANDWICH	Named for John Montagu, 4th Earl of Sandwich — the popular card-game legend is unverified. Solid: five years later, Captain James Cook named the Hawaiian Islands the "Sandwich Islands" in his honor Jan 20, 1778, since Montagu funded Cook's voyages as First Lord of the Admiralty.
e9365d27-5860-4c30-9556-8ed5274e574c	The Colosseum	The Romans never called it the Colosseum — it was the Flavian Amphitheatre. The nickname comes from the Colossus of Nero, a ~100-foot bronze statue of the emperor that stood next door; the name "Colosseum" only appears in medieval times, centuries after the statue itself had vanished (last recorded in the late 4th century). It's named after a statue that no longer existed.
ea256858-2b83-4d45-85ea-5e2f9dc5e723	Melon Heads	West Michigan legend holds that deformed "Melon Heads" escaped a real insane asylum near Felt Mansion outside Saugatuck after abuse. The Allegan County Historical Society states no such asylum ever existed there.
eaa4df51-cd90-40ee-ac91-3bacd14cff84	Shirley MacLaine	In a 1970 California Supreme Court case still taught in first-year contracts classes, Shirley MacLaine won full pay for a canceled musical after refusing 20th Century Fox's replacement offer, a western shot in Australia - the court ruled she didn't have to take a "different or inferior" job to collect damages.
eb1c169c-77b6-4737-8b90-8c071b29e225	Samoa	Samoa skipped December 30, 2011 entirely — clocks went from 11:59pm Dec 29 straight to midnight Dec 31 — jumping across the International Date Line to align with its biggest trading partners, Australia and New Zealand. It's the only country to have crossed the date line twice: in 1892 it shifted the other direction (repeating July 4 twice) to align with US West Coast trade instead.
ec5fd438-40a2-4b30-a55d-31de6c2c2b8a	The Blair Witch Project	During the film's first year (1999), IMDb listed its three lead actors as "missing, presumed dead," and the filmmakers handed out real missing-person flyers for them at Sundance asking people to call a local sheriff — the actors had to lie low for months while their families received condolence letters.
ecce0519-d08c-4c95-baf0-55f7915678fd	Giant's Causeway	Northern Ireland's famous 40,000 interlocking hexagonal basalt columns were formed by the exact same ancient lava flow, roughly 60 million years ago, as a nearly identical rock formation across the sea in Scotland — Fingal's Cave on the uninhabited isle of Staffa — split apart when the land between them eroded away.
ecfa6b3c-fd3b-4a0a-8f01-57d9c2a0fe0d	The Sound of Music	The real von Trapp family didn't hike over the Alps to Switzerland — geographically that route would have walked them straight into Nazi Germany. In reality they crossed the tracks behind their villa in broad daylight and took a train to Italy, where Georg von Trapp held citizenship because his birthplace, Zadar, had become Italian territory in 1920. Maria von Trapp herself complained after seeing the film: "Salzburg does not border on Switzerland!"
ecff8905-0aab-4df7-8da8-877f9e466c20	Lake Superior	Lake Superior is the world's largest freshwater lake by surface area — big enough to hold every other Great Lake plus three more the size of Lake Erie — holding roughly 10% of Earth's fresh surface water.
ed5e0ead-305d-48de-a971-78be3c390f05	Uniroyal Giant Tire	The 80-foot tire looming over I-94 in Allen Park, Michigan started life as a working Ferris wheel for Uniroyal's exhibit at the 1964–65 New York World's Fair, carrying 24 gondolas and over two million riders, including Jacqueline Kennedy and her children. After the fair, Uniroyal shipped it home in 188 pieces and reassembled it minus the ride mechanism.
ed96f08e-86e7-4caf-b6ed-aa1da2b4e2c7	TYPHOID MARY	Irish-American cook Mary Mallon was the first US-identified asymptomatic typhoid carrier — infected ~57 people (3 fatally), 1897-1907, cooking without ever showing symptoms. Forcibly quarantined on North Brother Island; released 1910, went back to cooking under a fake name, triggered a 1915 outbreak, confined there until her 1938 death.
ee2bd8dd-1055-41bb-b62d-92298565f55c	CIA	In the 1960s the CIA spent roughly $20 million on "Acoustic Kitty," a real project to surgically implant a live cat with a microphone, transmitter, and antenna to eavesdrop on Soviets. Cancelled in 1967 because handlers couldn't get cats to go where they wanted; declassified 2001.
ee39af63-e2d6-4aa5-9270-27680deb93f6	Patton (George S. Patton)	Before D-Day, the Allies gave Patton command of an army that didn't exist — the First United States Army Group (FUSAG), a paper formation of 150,000 imaginary men and thousands of inflatable tanks staged in Kent and Essex — specifically because German high command feared and respected him more than any other Allied general. Patton toured southern England giving public speeches to sell the ruse (Operation Fortitude/Quicksilver), helping convince the Germans the real target was Pas-de-Calais and holding their reserves there while the actual landings hit Normandy.
ee71858b-c927-426d-8c7e-12191d5b9f6c	ANGEL FALLS	At 979m, the world's tallest waterfall — named after American aviator Jimmie Angel, who spotted it flying over Venezuela in 1933, then crash-landed atop the tepui in 1937 and hiked 11 days back to civilization.
eeb099ab-3c2e-4d44-93f4-5e8568e44e55	Ian Fleming	In September 1940, British naval intelligence officer Ian Fleming proposed 'Operation Ruthless': deliberately crash a captured German bomber into the English Channel, fake an SOS, then ambush the German rescue boat sent to help and steal its Enigma codebooks. The plan was approved but scrapped when no suitable target traffic ever materialized.
f13e5cd9-2a9c-4b69-86f3-2282406ebfc5	Monopoly	Monopoly started as the opposite of what it became: Lizzie Magie patented "The Landlord's Game" in 1904 to demonstrate how landlords impoverish tenants, building in both a monopolist ruleset and an anti-monopolist one before Parker Brothers turned it into the capitalism-glorifying classic. Decades later, British intelligence unit MI9 used real Monopoly production runs to smuggle escape kits to WWII POWs — compasses, silk maps of German territory, and real foreign currency hidden under the play money, shipped in genuine-looking Red Cross-style parcels via manufacturer Waddington's.
f20c9da7-3eb0-4471-adba-fd409d32213e	Casey Kasem	Casey Kasem, longtime host of the national radio countdown American Top 40, was born in Detroit, Michigan in 1932.
f233f5d5-12c5-4741-9631-de3ce04825ef	Airplane!	The Zucker brothers and Jim Abrahams built their 1980 disaster-parody so closely on the 1957 drama Zero Hour! that they paid $2,500 for the official remake rights to head off a copyright claim.
f26cd0f9-2d8f-4eff-aec8-87a8de118764	Sting	Sting got his nickname as a teenager for wearing a yellow-and-black striped sweater that made a bandmate say he looked like a wasp — years before he co-founded The Police.
f3112841-3349-495f-8377-5a1ef8d561fd	Furby	In January 1999 the NSA banned Furbies from its Fort Meade headquarters, fearing the "learning" toy might record and repeat classified conversations — the ban spread to the Pentagon and a naval shipyard, then was quietly lifted once the toy was shown to have no recording hardware.
f31a9c1a-9f76-454e-a10e-86a0b7a5a124	CONNECT FOUR	Released by Milton Bradley in Feb. 1974, designed by Howard Wexler and Ned Strongin.
f3716d48-3492-41ad-a794-20268ea33c7a	TRIVIAL PURSUIT	"Trivia" traces to Latin trivium — "place where three roads meet." Crossroads gossip made trivialis mean "common, everyday" by the 1580s. The noun "trivia" wasn't coined until 1902 and didn't mean "fun useless facts" until 1960s college slang.
f3a51eb4-62af-4c91-ae8a-856d7e112c59	Dodge Brothers (Dodge v. Ford)	John and Horace Dodge were born in Niles, Michigan, and were among the original investors incorporating Ford Motor Company in 1903, becoming major stockholders before splitting off in 1914 to found their own car company.
f3b3eea9-64ac-43ba-9109-819b6634695e	Antarctica	Antarctica is technically the world's largest desert — its interior gets less than 2 inches of precipitation a year, drier than Death Valley — with zero permanent residents, just a rotating population of up to ~1,000 scientists.
f3fe0dd9-3784-42a2-ae24-3222d091b23f	NICKEL	German miners found copper-colored ore that yielded no copper and blamed a trickster spirit "Nickel" (nickname for Nikolaus, kin to England's "Old Nick" = the devil) — calling it *Kupfernickel*, "devil's copper." Swedish chemist Axel Cronstedt isolated the real element and kept the goblin's name.
f428ea18-dd3e-4e49-8f25-7145d17e93f4	Twinkies	MYTH: that Twinkies never expire / last forever. Hostess's own official shelf life is 45 days from the bakery — after that they go hard and stale like any other snack cake. A scientist who opened an 8-year-old Twinkie in 2020 described the result as tasting "like an old sock."
f4951417-f85b-41b4-99fa-401bac499137	Larry O'Brien Trophy	The NBA's championship trophy is named for a man who was, at various points, JFK's campaign manager, LBJ's Postmaster General, and NBA commissioner (1975–84) — and, as DNC chairman, the guy whose office at the Watergate complex was the actual target of the 1972 break-in that brought down Nixon's presidency.
f51e08b4-8687-48da-8214-051a8bc008d1	Domino's Pizza	Domino's Pizza started in 1960 when brothers Tom and Jim Monaghan borrowed $900 to buy a single pizza shop called DomiNick's in Ypsilanti, Michigan, near Eastern Michigan University. Tom later bought out his brother's half for a Volkswagen Beetle, renamed the business Domino's, and invented the corrugated insulated pizza box — still the industry standard — so more pizzas could stack per delivery run without crushing.
f524e6aa-cfff-4875-a084-09fa5d35ed4c	SILENT NIGHT	Christmas Eve 1914, German troops began singing "Stille Nacht" from their trenches; British soldiers sang back "Silent Night." By Christmas Day roughly 100,000 men — about two-thirds of troops in the area — had climbed into No Man's Land to trade gifts and play impromptu football.
f5a4a73c-52c8-4d5e-ba3a-2f67639f60ab	M&M's	M&M's exist because Forrest Mars Sr. watched soldiers during the Spanish Civil War eating chocolate pellets with a hard sugar shell that kept them from melting in the heat. He patented the idea in 1941, partnered with Hershey president's son Bruce Murrie for wartime chocolate access, and sold the candy exclusively to the U.S. military in ration kits until 1947 — when returning GIs made it a hit with the public.
f5c42628-9eaf-485a-99a7-68ed4df608ba	SILHOUETTE	Named for Étienne de Silhouette, France's finance minister for just 8 months in 1759, whose brutal austerity taxing nobility's luxuries made his name a byword for "cheap/skimpy" (*à la Silhouette*).
f5d7b3c3-5eae-4f7a-b356-c4418ad36e7c	OCTOPUS	An octopus has three hearts and blue blood — two pump blood through the gills, one circulates it through the body, using copper-based hemocyanin instead of iron-based hemoglobin. The body-heart stops beating when the octopus swims.
f6127075-13b8-4355-93b3-2660b3a03e95	Anastasia Romanov	After the 1918 execution of Russia's imperial family, rumors persisted that youngest daughter Anastasia survived. Top claimant Anna Anderson maintained it until her 1984 death; DNA testing proved she was a Polish factory worker. The mystery closed for good in 2007–2008 when scientists identified the remains of the last two missing Romanov children via DNA.
f63539dd-0a4c-46ba-99cc-d0111b56dc0b	JELLY BELLY	Ronald Reagan was such a devoted fan the company invented a new blueberry flavor for his 1981 inauguration so red/white/blue could be represented — in 1983 he sent Jelly Bellies aboard Space Shuttle Challenger mission STS-7, the first jelly beans in space.
f65c2dae-8980-46d9-9546-8ecb6b0baecd	Oasis	Oasis took its name from a poster for the Oasis Leisure Centre in Swindon, spotted by Noel Gallagher while working as a roadie for the Inspiral Carpets in 1991.
f74690d7-bb3e-453a-acb9-3c4d43ce027a	Willie Mays	Mays is the only player in MLB history to hit a home run in every inning from the 1st through the 16th. His 1st-inning and 16th-inning bookend homers were both off the same pitcher, Warren Spahn, the 16th-inning shot winning a 1-0 game 8 years later.
f75ded29-798d-4615-8be8-af6771418fa1	Honey	Honey is one of the only foods that genuinely never spoils if kept sealed and dry — archaeologists opening 3,000-year-old sealed jars in Egyptian tombs (including some recovered near Tutankhamun's burial goods) have found honey still edible. Its low water content, high acidity, and natural hydrogen peroxide make it hostile to the bacteria and mold that spoil everything else.
f79ec83d-4d20-4732-9983-2327c1dcff25	Candy Land	Candy Land was designed in 1948 by Eleanor Abbott while she was recovering from polio in a San Diego hospital, surrounded by children in the same ward — some in iron lungs. The game needs no reading and no strategy so bedridden kids could play. She gave much of her royalties to children's charities.
f7ecdd45-9b09-417a-b44d-1d752f5ce983	Creed Bratton	The Office's Creed isn't just named after the actor — Creed Bratton plays a fictionalized version of himself, and the writers built the character around his real backstory: in the 1960s he was the actual lead guitarist of The Grass Roots, a folk-rock band with real Billboard hits ("Midnight Confessions," "Let's Live for Today"). The show's version of Creed is written as "what if the real Creed had stayed on drugs and ended up passed out in a Scranton dumpster."
f8020374-9f8b-4c26-a698-4490d457f5ac	Cheetah	Zoo cheetahs are so chronically anxious that zoos assign them emotional support dogs. The San Diego Zoo pioneered it in the early 1980s, pairing a cheetah named Arusha with a golden retriever named Anna; cubs are matched with a puppy at ~3–4 months old, and the dog's calm body language teaches the cheetah nothing is a threat — the end goal being cheetahs confident enough to breed. The companion dogs are usually shelter rescues.
f81bed1c-a3a2-4294-81d7-dc4bfe0e36a9	Steven Seagal	After his April 20, 1991 hosting stint, he was banned from SNL — and the show said so on air: in Nicolas Cage's 1992 monologue, Cage worried viewers would think him "the biggest jerk who's ever been on the show," and Lorne Michaels replied, "No, no. That would be Steven Seagal." The episode was pulled from syndication.
f9bffd37-6192-4afe-ae7a-ca7846df8bb1	Eugene Shoemaker	Geologist Eugene Shoemaker is the only human being interred on the Moon — after he died in a 1997 car crash, NASA carried an ounce of his ashes aboard the Lunar Prospector probe, which deliberately crash-landed into a lunar crater in 1998. Shoemaker himself had failed the astronaut medical exam (Addison's disease) and never got to go there alive.
fa23abde-336f-486a-b939-bd7e2fd1cb19	Mr. Monopoly's monocle	MYTH: most people confidently remember the Monopoly Man wearing a monocle. He never has, in any official art since his 1936 debut — it's a textbook "Mandela Effect," studied by University of Chicago researchers alongside similar false memories about Pikachu's tail and the Fruit of the Loom logo.
fae1d3da-55f6-4c82-81dd-c00664b3581e	Bad Company	Bad Company's name is widely credited to the 1972 Jeff Bridges western of the same title, but singer Paul Rodgers says that's wrong: he took it from a Victorian morality-book illustration captioned "Beware of bad company," and says he never even saw the movie.

fb781a77-87e5-488e-a091-fd21c6544ce3	Deadpool	Deadpool's civilian name, "Wade Wilson," is an inside joke — co-creator Fabian Nicieza gave him that name because Rob Liefeld's 1990 design looked so much like DC's Deathstroke, whose real name is Slade Wilson.
fc058d46-731c-490d-bbe5-069b02d7cdee	THE LITTLE MERMAID (Ursula)	Ursula went through failed early designs as a manta ray and a scorpionfish before animator Rob Minkoff sketched a "vampy, overweight matron" the team agreed "looked a lot like Divine" — the drag performer and John Waters muse.
fc14d333-fe81-4c50-a712-222e2bd43162	2025 Ig Nobel Prize (drunk bats study)	The 2025 Ig Nobel Prize for Aviation went to a team of researchers for feeding Egyptian fruit bats alcohol-soaked food and clocking how much it slowed their flying and garbled their echolocation calls — essentially proving bats can fly drunk, badly, which is why they avoid overripe, fermented fruit in the wild.
fc290d43-ad3d-4680-a240-b53f5cd88d5f	BOYCOTT	In 1880, English land agent Capt. Charles Boycott managed 1,500 acres in County Mayo, Ireland. When he evicted tenants after a rent dispute, the Irish Land League got locals to shun him totally. Journalist James Redpath coined the verb "to boycott" that October.
fc6cd675-d83a-4902-b2c2-db4e290048bd	Stan Lee	Born Stanley Martin Lieber, he racked up roughly 45 cameo appearances across Marvel-related films — 23 in the MCU alone, Iron Man (2008) through a posthumous appearance in Deadpool & Wolverine (2024).
fca2354c-87b5-4e5f-a6b3-27eca650754c	Sweden	On "Dagen H" (H-Day), September 3 1967, all of Sweden switched from driving on the left side of the road to the right — every driver in the country pulled over at 4:50am and resumed on the opposite side at 5:00am sharp. Fatal car accidents dropped sharply afterward.
fcbb8f50-e964-48f1-aeed-f04702889704	Monopoly	Monopoly was invented by Elizabeth Magie in 1903 as The Landlord's Game — designed to teach the DANGERS of monopolies (Georgist economics). Parker Brothers bought her patent for $500, and Charles Darrow got the credit (and the fortune) for decades. The truth only surfaced publicly via a 1973 lawsuit over a game called Anti-Monopoly.
fce0a657-59bb-405f-aaa9-1486d46c1aef	Heisman Trophy	The famous straight-arm pose on the trophy isn't John Heisman — it's Ed Smith, an NYU running back who modeled for sculptor Frank Eliscu in 1934 and stayed unaware he was on the most famous trophy in college football for 48 years, until a documentary filmmaker found him in 1982.
fd438b44-7ce6-4e7c-aedb-3dd54b4f19cb	blink-182	The band's own official history says "182" was picked essentially at random one night while Tom DeLonge and Mark Hoppus were skateboarding around Poway, California — forced into a rename after an Irish band already called "Blink" threatened to sue. The widely repeated claim that 182 is the number of times "fuck" is said in Scarface is false: even ex-member Matt Skiba has publicly debunked it, and the real count lands somewhere in the low-to-mid 200s.
fd69e87a-934d-4267-b051-c124082e3812	Andre Geim	Physicist Andre Geim is the only person to win both a Nobel Prize and an Ig Nobel Prize. He took home the joke prize in 2000 for levitating a live frog with magnets, then the real Nobel Prize in Physics in 2010 for isolating graphene, a material one atom thick.
fdbd2136-2278-4351-a2bf-47d8c1ad21ce	Scrabble	Scrabble's letter values were set in the 1930s by unemployed architect Alfred Butts, who hand-counted letter frequency on the front page of the New York Times (plus the Herald Tribune and Saturday Evening Post). He included only four S tiles specifically so plurals wouldn't make the game too easy. His counts are still the distribution used today.
fee2402d-0ebe-47a3-9248-a9b707de895a	Van Halen	Van Halen's 1982 tour rider demanded a bowl of M&M's backstage with "ABSOLUTELY NO BROWN ONES" — not diva excess but a deliberate trap: the technical rider ran dozens of pages of stage-weight and safety specs, and a stray brown M&M proved the venue hadn't read it.

When the studio kept his name on the home-video release anyway, King sued again.
ff337dd3-5c52-4d47-b2e6-14161ff202cb	EDDIE THE EAGLE EDWARDS	Britain's first Olympic ski jumper finished dead last in both events at the 1988 Calgary Games — by a landslide — becoming a global sensation the closing ceremony shouted him out.
ffd669f1-ad3c-487d-9e54-9f44095a6d5d	PRINGLES	Chemist Fredric Baur spent two years designing Pringles' saddle-shaped chip and tubular can for P&G in the 1960s. When he died in 2008 at 89, his family honored his wish and buried part of his cremated remains inside an Original-flavor Pringles can.
fff607fa-96cb-4cac-999b-6b4f7bb704ac	Nice	"Nice" meant "foolish, ignorant, senseless" when it entered English around 1300, from Latin "nescius" (not-knowing) — it didn't pick up its modern "pleasant" meaning until the 1700s.
